NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

I. Executive Summary
1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
Aficamten is an allosteric and reversible inhibitor of cardiac myosin. Aficamten reduces the
force generated by myosin at the cardiac sarcomere which reduces cardiac contractility. The
Applicant, Cytokinetics, submitted NDA 219083 for aficamten to support the proposed
indication, “treatment of adults with symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiopathy
(oHCM).” NDA 219083 relies on data from a single adequate and well-controlled trial,
SEQUOIA-HCM, to support the proposed indication along with confirmatory evidence from the
phase 2 REDWOOD-HCM study.
All review disciplines, the Cross Disciplinary Team Leader, the deputy director of the Division
of Cardiology and Nephrology, and the signatory concur that the evidence submitted to NDA
219083 satisfy the statutory requirement for substantial evidence of effectiveness to support the
approval of aficamten for the indication to improve exercise capacity and reduce symptoms in
patients with symptomatic oHCM, and that these benefits outweigh aficamten’s risks.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
SEQUOIA-HCM Trial
SEQUOIA-HCM was a Phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, and placebo-controlled
trial that evaluated the efficacy and safety of aficamten in subjects with symptomatic oHCM.
The trial included a screening period of up to 6 weeks, a double-blind, placebo-controlled
treatment period of 24 weeks, and a 4-week safety follow-up period. A total of 282 eligible
subjects were randomized 1:1 to aficamten or placebo at 82 sites in 14 countries. The starting
and maximum doses of aficamten were 5 mg and 20 mg orally once daily with or without food,
respectively.
The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in peak oxygen consumption
(pVO2) at Week 24 (significance level: 2-sided alpha 0.05). Secondary efficacy endpoints, listed
as per the statistical testing hierarchy all at the significance level of 2-sided alpha 0.05 were:
1. Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire-Clinical Summary Score (KCCQ-CSS) change
from baseline at Week 24.
2. Proportion of subjects with >1 New York Heart Association (NYHA) functional class
improvement at Week 24.
3. Valsalva left ventricular outflow tract gradient (LVOT-G) change from baseline at Week 24.
3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
4. Proportion of subjects with valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 24.
5. Duration of septal reduction therapy (SRT) eligibility up to 24 weeks of treatment for those
who were SRT at baseline.
6. KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 12.
7. Proportion of subjects with >1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 12.
8. Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 12.
9. Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 12.
10. Change from baseline in total workload during cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET).
Baseline characteristics were similar between the treatment groups.
The pVO2 primary endpoint result showed a least-squares (LS) mean difference (95% confidence
interval) of 1.7 (1.0, 2.4) for aficamten compared to placebo, p-value<0.0001. The treatment
effect on the primary endpoint was generally consistent across various predefined subgroups
based on demographic characteristics, baseline disease characteristics, and concomitant
medications.
The results of all the secondary efficacy endpoints listed above were also statistically significant.
The clinical utility of pVO2 is the ability to assess functional capacity and the effectiveness of an
intervention in cardiomyopathy trials, and thus, it is an accepted endpoint for a regulatory
decision. The observed treatment effect on pVO2 was interpreted to be clinically meaningful (see
Section 6.3.1). This finding was accompanied by a large treatment effect of aficamten on LVOTG as well as clinically meaningful improvement on functional capacity and symptoms assessed
with the KCCQ-CSS and NYHA functional class.
In summary, the SEQUOIA-HCM trial provided evidence from an adequate and well controlled
clinical investigation that aficamten improves functional capacity and symptoms in subjects with
symptomatic oHCM as measured by pVO2, KCCQ-CSS and NYHA class.
REDWOOD-HCM Study
REDWOOD-HCM was a Phase 2, multi-center, randomized, placebo-controlled, dose-finding
study that evaluated the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of
aficamten in adults with symptomatic oHCM. The study was performed at 23 centers in Italy,
Netherlands, Spain, and the United States. Subjects with oHCM and LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg at rest
or ≥50 mm Hg with Valsalva were randomized 2:1 to receive aficamten (n=28) or placebo
(n=13) in 2 dose-finding cohorts (i.e., 14 subjects per dose-finding cohort). Doses were titrated,
up to 3 escalating doses, based on LVOT-G and left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF).
Efficacy was evaluated by echocardiography (i.e., LVOT-G, LVEF), NYHA class, and patientreported outcome quality of life assessment. Results showed a clinically meaningful reduction in
LVOT-G in the combined aficamten cohorts compared to placebo, at rest (-25 mm Hg) and postValsalva (-41 mm Hg) at Week 10, providing confirmatory evidence of effectiveness of
aficamten to reduce LVOT-G in symptomatic oHCM subjects.

oHCM is a rare, chronic, progressive disease
 Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM) is a rare genetic myocardial disorder
characterized by cardiomyocyte disarray,
characterized by left ventricular (LV) hypertrophy, hyperdynamic contraction,
hypercontractile myocardium, and impaired
and impaired relaxation.

 The prevalence of asymptomatic HCM among young adults in the United
presentations and clinical course. oHCM is
States has been estimated at 1 in 200 to 1 in 500, with symptomatic HCM
being less common based on medical claims data (estimated at lower than 1 in associated with significant cardiovascularrelated morbidity and mortality, including
3,000).
 About 70% of individuals diagnosed with HCM have obstructive HCM (oHCM) functional limitation and risk of sudden
cardiac death.
with a prevalence of 2-5 per 100,000 people. The presence of left ventricular
outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction is an important prognostic factor in HCM.
 Complications of oHCM include arrhythmias, dilated cardiomyopathy, mitral
regurgitation, heart failure (HF), stroke, and sudden death from ventricular
tachycardia/ventricular fibrillation. The incidence of sudden death is
approximately 1% per year, often in people <30 years of age. The cause of
death from oHCM is age-dependent (sudden death in the younger population,
HF in middle age, and stroke in the elderly).
 Patients with oHCM have a wide range of symptoms and disease courses. The
typical symptoms of oHCM include dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance,
angina, palpitations, and syncope. Some patients suffer from high symptom
burden causing significant debilitating consequences, thus profoundly affecting
their quality of life.

Conclusions and Reasons

The effectiveness of aficamten for the treatment of adults with oHCM was
evaluated in the SEQUOIA-HCM trial, a randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled, multicenter, international, parallel-group study in patients with
symptomatic oHCM.
The primary efficacy endpoint was change from baseline in pVO 2 at Week 24.
At baseline, mean pVO2 was 18.5 mL/kg/min. The study met its primary
endpoint showing greater increase in pVO2 with aficamten compared to
placebo with a LS mean difference (95% confidence interval) of 1.7 (1.0, 2.4),
p-value<0.0001.
The clinical meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on pVO2 of
1.7 mL/min/kg between groups was supported by the following exploratory
analyses:
– The empirical cumulative density function curves of changes in pVO2 by
NYHA class groups

Risk and risk 
management



Safety evaluation of aficamten was primarily based on the data obtained in
the SEQUOIA-HCM trial (N=282 receiving at least one dose of study drug;
n=142 in the aficamten group and n=140 in the placebo group). This study
provided controlled data with a median exposure of 6 months. Long-term
safety is supported by the open-label extension Study CY 6022. With the
updated safety data through August 31, 2024, the median exposure for all
aficamten-treated patients was about 11 months.
Safety evaluation focused on the on-target effects of aficamten, whereby
reduction of contractility could increase the risk of HF with reduced ejection
fraction.
– There was a small decrease in LVEF (~4 to 5% with an SD of 7%) during
24 weeks of aficamten treatment compared with placebo. LVEF decline
appeared reversible with drug discontinuation.
– The incidence of LVEF <50% was low (4 to 5% assessed by core
laboratory or site-read), and none of the subjects who experienced LVEF
<50% had associated signs and symptoms of HF except one with
reported adverse event (AE) of HF 4 weeks prior to the reduction of
LVEF.
– No aficamten-treated oHCM patients experienced a serious HF event or
severe systolic dysfunction (LVEF <30%) in SEQUOIA-HCM and the
extension study.
– Hypertension was the only AE that occurred at a frequency of ≥5%
higher in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group (8% versus
2%).
– There was a greater mean change from baseline in both systolic and
diastolic blood pressure in the aficamten group versus placebo group at

Reference ID: 5715605

The principal safety concern of aficamten is
the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction
resulting from an excessive pharmacologic
effect of aficamten. In SEQUOIA-HCM, in
which regular pharmacodynamic (PD)
monitoring via echocardiogram was
implemented, the risk of HF and/or systolic
dysfunction was low during 28 weeks of
treatment. The risk remained low in the
ongoing extension study in patients with
oHCM.
We determined that an elements to assure
safe use (ETASU) REMS is required to
assure safe use of aficamten for the following
reasons: (1) an iatrogenic risk of HF with
aficamten exists based on the mechanism of
action of the drug; (2) the total exposure to
aficamten to date remains relatively low
(~300 oHCM patients); and (3) the risk is
likely to be higher in the postmarket
compared to trial setting (in the postmarket
setting aficamten is likely to be used in a
wider range of patients with higher
comorbidities and where clinical monitoring is
less rigorous).

 Concomitant use of potent inhibitors of multiple CYP enzymes, including
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4, such as fluconazole and fluvoxamine,
with aficamten may increase aficamten exposure and increase the risk of
HF and/or systolic dysfunction. Dose modification is required when
concurrent use of these drugs occurs.
 There are insufficient human pregnancy data in the aficamten-exposed
subjects to inform the safety of aficamten use in pregnancy. The animal
reproductive toxicity data do not suggest a serious risk of developmental
toxicity during pregnancy.

Conclusions and Reasons
The only observed adverse reaction with
aficamten was hypertension. Aficamtenassociated increases in blood pressure are
consistent with the hemodynamic
consequence of attenuating the LVOT
obstruction. This risk can be managed
through labeling.
To further assess safety of aficamten during
pregnancy, we will issue a postmarketing
requirement for a descriptive pregnancy
safety study and lactation study to collect
prospective and retrospective data in women
exposed to aficamten during pregnancy
and/or lactation.

MYQORZO (aficamten)

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
In the phase 3 trial, aficamten improved peak oxygen consumption (an assessment of functional
capacity) over 24 weeks compared to placebo in adults with oHCM. While the mean treatment
effect size appears modest (i.e., treatment difference of 1.7 mL/kg/min at Week 24 from a
baseline mean of 18.5 mL/kg/min), these changes are accompanied by a large reduction in
LVOT-G (mean treatment effect of -48 mmHg on Valsalva LVOT-G at Week 24 from a baseline
mean of 83 mmHg) as well as benefits on patient symptoms and functional capacity assessed
using KCCQ-CSS (mean treatment effect of 7 from a baseline mean of ~75) and NYHA
functional class (59% improvement with aficamten at Week 24 compared to 24% improvement
with placebo). We conclude that aficamten clearly improves symptoms and functional capacity
in adults with oHCM.
Due to aficamten’s mechanism of action the main concern is a risk of precipitating HF, although
the risk appears low when echocardiography is used to monitor LVEF and guide dose titration.
There is also drug-drug interaction potential, which can be managed by adjusting the aficamten
dose and/or avoiding coadministration.
Aficamten’s benefits on patient symptoms and functional capacity outweigh the risk of HF when
the risk is mitigated with an ETASU REMS, which will help ensure patients on aficamten have
regular echocardiograms to monitor for reduced LVEF and guide dose titration. The drug-drug
interaction potential can be adequately mitigated with labeling, that describes the scenarios for
dose adjustment and/or avoidance of aficamten. There is a risk of hypertension, consistent with
the reduced LVOT, that can also be adequately managed with labeling, informing prescribers of
this risk, which can be assessed with periodic blood pressure monitoring (which is routine in
clinical care) and treated, as needed. Patients will also be informed of these risks through a
Medication Guide, which is required to be dispensed with each new prescription and refill.
Based on these considerations, we conclude that the overall benefit-risk assessment is favorable
for aficamten for the treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM to improve functional capacity
and symptoms and that aficamten should be approved.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
NDA 219083 relies on a single, Phase 3 trial, SEQUOIA-HCM (CY 6031) and a Phase 2, dosefinding study, REDWOOD-HCM to support the following proposed indication for aficamten:
“AFICAMTEN is a cardiac myosin inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adults with
symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (oHCM)”
The Applicant also has an ongoing long-term, single-arm, open-label extension study to assess
the safety and durability of aficamten in patients with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM).
Disease Background (Autore et al. 2005; Maron et al. 2006; Ommen et al. 2024)
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is an autosomal dominant genetic heart disease that affects men
and women globally and is characterized by left ventricular (LV) hypertrophy, hyperdynamic
contraction and impaired relaxation. The prevalence of asymptomatic HCM among young adults
in the United States has been estimated to be approximately 1 in 500, with symptomatic HCM
based on medical claims data being less common (estimated at <1 in 3000) (1). A large
percentage of patients are asymptomatic and/or undiagnosed.
There are two HCM phenotypes based on the presence or absence of obstruction of the LVOT,
oHCM and non-obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (nHCM), where obstruction is defined
as a peak LVOT gradient ≥30 mm Hg at rest or ≥50 mm Hg with provocation (e.g., Valsalva
maneuver). Approximately 70% of individuals diagnosed with HCM have oHCM (2). The health
impacts of HCM are variable with a wide range of symptoms and disease courses.
Many patients experience minimal or few symptoms and minor or no limitations in daily
activities; however, others suffer significant debilitating consequences and high symptom
burden, most frequently dyspnea, chest pain and palpitations with profound effects on their lives.
The presence of LVOT obstruction is an important prognostic factor in HCM (3). HCM-related
adverse outcomes include sudden death, progressively limiting symptoms caused by diastolic
dysfunction and/or LVOT obstruction, heart failure (HF) caused by systolic dysfunction, and
thromboembolic complications.
First-line, guideline-directed pharmacologic management of oHCM includes beta-blockers and
non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers. Patients with oHCM who do not respond to firstline therapy are candidates for advanced pharmacologic therapy including disopyramide and
cardiac myosin inhibitors. The first-in-class and only approved cardiac myosin inhibitor is
mavacamten, approved by the FDA in 2022 for treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM to
improve functional capacity and symptoms. However, mavacamten has substantial drug-drug
interactions (DDIs) and a long half-life that increase the risk of systolic dysfunction and heart
failure. Mavacamten is available only through a REMS program. Septal reduction therapy with
either surgical myectomy or alcohol septal ablation is generally reserved for drug-refractory
symptoms or intolerant adverse effects from pharmacologic therapy. Although SRT is effective,
it is only available in experienced HCM centers and there are potential procedural complications,
such as spill-over (alcohol infusion into other arteries causing an expanded myocardial
10
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
infarction), disruption of electrical conduction causing blocks and potential pacemaker
requirements, and resurgence of LVOT obstruction requiring a repeat procedure. There continues
to be an unmet need for a disease-specific drug to treat symptomatic oHCM.
Drug Class
Aficamten (also referred to as CK-3773274), is a small molecule, allosteric and reversible
inhibitor of cardiac myosin. Aficamten reduces the force generated by myosin at the cardiac
sarcomere. In oHCM patients, myosin inhibition with aficamten reduces cardiac sarcomere
contractility and LVOT obstruction.

MYQORZO (aficamten)

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
5.1.1. Mechanism of Action
Aficamten is a small molecule inhibitor of cardiac myosin intended for the treatment of adults
with symptomatic oHCM. oHCM is characterized by left ventricular hypertrophy and
hypercontractility, with impaired relaxation and filling during diastole. This causes abnormal
hemodynamics and clinical symptoms such as chest pain, fatigue, and syncope. By specifically
inhibiting cardiac myosin, a sarcomeric molecular motor in cardiomyocytes, aficamten reduces
the excessive contractility that contributes to the pathophysiology of oHCM.
At a molecular level, cardiomyocyte contractility is driven by the cyclic interaction between
myosin and actin at the cardiac sarcomere. Myosin and actin interactions are regulated by
calcium, the presence of which allows myosin’s motor head domain to bind actin. Subsequent
adenosine triphosphate (ATP) hydrolysis powers sarcomere shortening and cardiomyocyte
contraction (Figure 1). The myosin-actin complex dissociates after each power stroke, which
primes the system for another round of contraction. Aficamten binds directly to the head domain
of cardiac myosin and is more selective for the cardiac β-myosin heavy chain isoform
(Moussavi-Harami and Regnier 2024), which is predominantly expressed in the ventricles.
Aficamten binding stabilizes myosin in an off-actin conformation, decreasing the number of
functional myosin heads entering a force-generating state, resulting in reduced cardiomyocyte
contractility (Figure 1).
Figure 1. Schematic Illustration of the Mechanism of Action of Aficamten in Obstructive
Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy

Source: Adopted from (Maron et al. 2023)
Abbreviations: HCM, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; HF, heart failure; LVOT, left ventricular outflow tract; QoL, quality of life

17
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

5.1.2. In Vitro and In Vivo Pharmacology
Experiments in vitro with myofibrils from various muscle types across species and cultured
cardiomyocytes interrogated the primary pharmacology and selectivity of aficamten. Detailed
summaries of individual studies are provided in Section 13.1 of this review. Aficamten decreased
the ATPase activity of bovine cardiac and slow muscle myofibrils with similar half maximal
inhibitory concentration (IC50) values, due to the same myosin isoform (MYH7) being expressed
in each muscle type but was a weak inhibitor of both rabbit fast muscle myofibrils and chicken
gizzard smooth muscle (see Table 48). These results show that aficamten spares smooth muscle
and fast-twitch skeletal muscle contractility at concentrations that inhibit cardiac and slow-twitch
skeletal muscle contractility in vitro. Notably, no signals related to skeletal muscle weakness
were observed in in vivo nonclinical studies or in clinical trials. Cardiac myosin proteins are
highly evolutionarily conserved, allowing comparison of data across myofibril preparations from
different species, which allows for clinically relevant nonclinical pharmacology studies in
animals. Aficamten inhibits contractility without impacting intracellular cardiomyocyte calcium
concentrations, demonstrated in vitro in both rat ventricular cardiomyocytes and human
cardiomyocytes derived from induced pluripotent stem cells, suggesting low risk for arrythmias
with clinical use. Aficamten potency was similar in engineered heart tissues derived from
induced pluripotent stem cells from a normal donor (IC50=1.08µM) and those from a donor
heterozygous for MYH7 R403Q (IC50=1.65µM), demonstrating its efficacy against a cardiac
myosin mutation associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and its potential to treat HCM of
multiple etiologies.
Prospect of clinical benefit was shown using heterozygous transgenic MYH7 R403Q mice with
HCM, and a cat model of hereditary oHCM (A31P MYBPC3 mutation), using aficamten doses
comparable to those used in toxicology studies. Notably, the transgenic mouse develops LV
thickening without LVOT obstruction, while the cat model reliably reflects human oHCM
symptoms including LVOT obstruction. Aficamten dose-dependently reduced left ventricular
fractional shortening (LVFS) compared to baseline after a single dose in both disease models. In
vivo pharmacology studies were also conducted in healthy rats and dogs without HCM. In
healthy rats, IC10 (the plasma concentration to result in a 10% reduction in LVFS) was observed
at 270 ng/mL and IC50 at 2,662 ng/mL. In healthy dogs, the IC10 was observed at 60 ng/mL and
IC50 at 430 ng/mL, with a slightly steeper dose response than rats.
There was no major additional magnitude of the pharmacodynamic (PD) effect in healthy rats
between a single dose or repeated dosing with aficamten, and the effects reversed 24 hours after
the final dose. Reduced LVFS caused by aficamten was rapidly rescued in rats by administration
of dobutamine, a β-adrenergic agonist, demonstrating a pharmacological method to reverse its
action. The pharmacodynamics of aficamten was not significantly impacted in a heterozygous
MYH7 R403Q mouse model of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, as the dose response relationship
was similar to that observed in wild-type mice. Aficamten decreased the LVOT pressure gradient
in a cat model of oHCM after a single dose, indicating improvement of mitral valve obstruction
coinciding with reduced LVFS.
Taken together, these data provided a therapeutic rationale for relieving LVOT obstruction in
oHCM subjects treated with aficamten. Aficamten was shown to reversibly (non-covalently)
inhibit the contractility of purified myofibrils, intact cultured cardiomyocytes, and hearts in vivo.
Aficamten acts at the sarcomere, independently of electrophysiology. Aficamten was similarly
18
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
potent between cardiomyocytes with wild type myosin and R403Q mutants, one of the most
common clinical mutations associated with HCM. Aficamten had no appreciable activity against
smooth muscle or skeletal fast-twitch muscle at clinically relevant concentrations but may
theoretically have unintended effects on skeletal slow-twitch muscle myofibrils, although no
safety signal has been identified. Aficamten reduced LVFS and LVOT pressure gradient in a cat
model of oHCM, providing rationale for its development as a therapeutic.
Overall, the nonclinical data submitted by the Applicant support the proposed therapeutic
rationale for the development of aficamten for oHCM and provide adequate information to label
its mechanism of action (Section 12.1 of the product prescribing information [PI]).

5.1.3. Established Pharmacologic Class and Rationale
For Established Pharmacologic Class of aficamten, the Applicant proposed “cardiac myosin
inhibitor,” which was used for Camzyos (mavacamten).
Several lines of in vitro and in vivo evidence suggest that aficamten binds to the cardiac myosin
motor and decreases its ATPase activity, providing a molecular basis of reducing cardiac
contractility to treat hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Thus, the use of “cardiac myosin inhibitor” as
the Established Pharmacologic Class is supported. Aficamten would be the second-in-class
cardiac myosin inhibitor, should it be approved. Mavacamten, the first-in-class, was approved for
hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in 2023.

Mechanism of action
Aficamten is an allosteric and reversible inhibitor of cardiac myosin motor activity. Aficamten reduces the force
generated by myosin at the cardiac sarcomere, which contributes to the pathophysiology of hypertrophic
cardiomyopathy (HCM). In HCM subjects, myosin inhibition with aficamten may reduce cardiac sarcomere

Drug Information
When 20 mg (100 μCi) of 14C-aficamten was administered to healthy males, approximately 89.7% of the radioactive
oral dose was recovered, with 32.0% excreted in urine and 57.7% excreted in feces. Aficamten was eliminated
primarily by metabolism and, to a lesser extent, by fecal excretion, with renal excretion playing a minor role (Study
CY 6013).
The population PK-derived clearance (CL/F) estimate of aficamten in subjects with oHCM was 2.62 L/hr (Population
PK report CYT AFI-PMX-001).
Aficamten has a terminal elimination half-life of approximately 80 hours (Population PK report CYT-AFI-PMX-001).
Aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9-mediated metabolism (50%) with contributions from CYP3A (26%),
CYP2D6, and CYP2C19 (Studies Nonclin-0029 and Nonclin-0451).
See Mass balance study results above.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
Body weight is predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten plasma AUCtau, Cmax,
and Ctau in a subject with oHCM at the fourth (i.e., highest) weight quartile were 34%, 35%, and 33% lower,
respectively, relative to a subject with oHCM at the first (i.e., lowest) weight quartile (Population PK report CYT-AFIPMX-001). No dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on body weight.
Age
Age was not identified as a statistically significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics (Population PK report
CYT-AFI-PMX-001).
Renal impairment
No impact of mild or moderate renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics was identified in population PK
analysis based on the Phase 2 (Study CY 6021) and Phase 3 (Trial CY 6031) data (Population PK report CYT-AFIPMX-001).
Hepatic impairment
The exposure (i.e., Cmax and AUC) of aficamten were similar (i.e., no meaningful difference) in subjects with normal
liver function and subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (i.e., Child-Pugh Class B) (Study CY 6017). No studies
were conducted in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (i.e., Child-Pugh Class C).
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Precipitant)

metabolism
Nonclin-0052). Aficamten is not predicted to be an in vivo inducer in humans (Studies Nonclin-0016 and Nonclin0494).

Aficamten is a weak P-gp inhibitor (Study CY 6014)

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Applicant’s Dose Selection for Phase 3 Trial
The clinical development program sought to maintain a plasma concentration of aficamten
required for desired efficacy, but avoiding supratherapeutic concentrations that may result in
systolic dysfunction, leading to decrease in stroke volume and cardiac output, compensatory
increases in heart rate, and potentially clinical heart failure. Therefore, the goal of dose selection
was to find the lowest possible dose to safely achieve resting and Valsalva LVOT-G targets (i.e.,
resting LVOT-G of <30 mm Hg and Valsalva LVOT-G of <50 mm Hg) while maintaining
normal LVEF (i.e., LVEF ≥50%).
Dose selection for the Phase 3 development program was determined from a Phase 2,
multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study in subjects with
oHCM (Study CY 6021, REDWOOD-HCM). The REDWOOD-HCM study had two dosing
regimen cohorts and one placebo cohort. Subjects with symptomatic oHCM were randomized in
a 2:1 ratio to aficamten or placebo and received up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (i.e., 5, 10,
and 15 mg in Cohort 1 and 10, 20, and 30 mg in Cohort 2) or placebo once daily for 10 weeks.
Dose escalation in the aficamten cohorts was based on echocardiographic guidance. Subjects
maintained background therapy including beta-blockers and/or calcium channel blockers, either
as monotherapy or combined, but excluding the use of disopyramide given its unique safety
profile and the potential impacts on the studied outcomes.
Study CY 6021 was conducted with an objective to treat with the lowest possible dose to safely
achieve resting and Valsalva LVOT-G targets and maintain normal LVEF. The 5 mg starting
dose was demonstrated to be pharmacodynamically effective and was well-tolerated by all
subjects. There were 11 out of 41 subjects from Cohorts 1 and 2 who required doses higher than
10 mg to achieve target LVOT-G. Two subjects were escalated up to 30 mg (Table 8) but one of
the two subjects on the 30-mg dose was down-titrated to 20 mg due to LVEF <50%, and the
other subjects did not achieve an adequate PD response. Based on these data the Applicant
concluded that the 30-mg dose is not likely to add substantial clinical benefit versus risk;
therefore, 20 mg was selected as the highest dose in the Phase 3 trial. The Applicant’s dose
selection for the Phase 3 trial was deemed reasonable.

Evaluation of the Phase 3 Trial Dosage Regimen
The Phase 3 trial (CY 6031, SEQUOIA-HCM) dose regimen was evaluated using two
complementary methods. First, exposure-response (E-R) analyses were performed to identify and
quantify the relationship between steady-state aficamten exposure derived from population
pharmacokinetic (PK) modeling and key primary, secondary, and exploratory efficacy and safety
endpoints (see Section 14.5.2). Second, PK/PD modeling of the relationship between aficamten
pharmacokinetics and the primary echocardiographic measurements for dose titration, Valsalva
LVOT-G, and LVEF, was performed (see Section 14.5.1)
The Applicant’s population PK (PPK) model described the aficamten serum concentration-time
profiles adequately following the administration of aficamten in subjects with oHCM. The PPK
model was assessed as acceptable for simulating post hoc exposure metrics, e.g., area under the
concentration-time curve (AUC)tau, maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), and trough plasma
concentration (Ctau) of aficamten for PK/PD and E-R analyses of efficacy and safety
measurements. The PK/PD model of aficamten was adequate to link aficamten exposure to
LVEF and LVOT-G for aficamten-treated subjects from the REDWOOD-HCM study and Trial
CY 6031. The results were used to support the proposed commercial dose regimen. The
pharmacometrics reviewer conducted independent analyses to evaluate the frequency of
echocardiographic assessment during titration and maintenance phases. The final PK/PD analysis
is comprised of a curve with LVEF or LVOT-G declining in a slightly exponential manner with
increasing aficamten exposure and is supportive of the proposed dose titration scheme. Refer to
the following section below. Except for the probability of ≥1 NYHA class improvement at Week
24 and change in N-terminal pro-B-type natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) at Week 24, the results
of exposure-efficacy analyses did not show a significant E-R relationship in the aficamtentreated group. The results of exposure-safety analyses did not show a trend between aficamten
exposure (Cavg,ss and Cmax,ss) and safety endpoints such as major adverse cardiovascular events,
cardiovascular adverse events, or hypertension (please refer to Section 14.5).
In the Phase 3 trial, CY 6031, the efficacy and safety for aficamten 5 mg - 20 mg has been
adequately demonstrated. In conclusion, the Applicant’s selection of the Phase 3 dosage regimen
was appropriate.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Dose Titration Scheme
Phase 2 and 3 Development
In both Phase 2 and 3 studies, an individualized echocardiography-guided dose titration strategy
was consistently used to maximize achievement of target LVOT-G and maintain LVEF ≥50%.
For subjects with oHCM in the Phase 2 study (CY 6021), three opportunities for dose titration
were provided (i.e., Weeks 2, 4, and 6). Dose titration proceeded until the reduction of the
Valsalva LVOT-G to <50 mm Hg and resting LVOT-G to <30 mm Hg. Doses were adjusted to
the maximum protocol-specified dose if LVEF ≥50%. If LVEF was <50% at any time, the
subjects was returned to the prior dose level or placed on placebo if the subjects was on the
lowest dose level (i.e., 5 mg in Cohort 1 and 10 mg in Cohort 2).
The Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) followed a similar dose titration strategy as the Phase 2 study (CY
6021); however, the LVOT-G target criteria for dose titration was simplified by focusing on the
Valsalva LVOT-G only. The Valsalva LVOT-G target was reduced from 50 mm Hg to 30 mm
Hg to maximize the potential therapeutic benefit of aficamten. In addition, the lower limit of
LVEF for dose escalation was increased from 50% to 55% to provide a safety margin from the
threshold of LVEF (<50%) that triggered dose reduction in both studies. If LVEF was <50% at
any time, the subjects was returned to the prior dose level, or to placebo if the subject was on
5 mg. Subjects were able to receive up to 4 increasing dose levels (i.e., 5, 10, 15, and 20 mg) of
aficamten over 3 dose titration opportunities. Doses of aficamten were individually titrated at
Weeks 2, 4, and 6 using echocardiography, as outlined in Table 9. Dose escalation occurred only
if a subject had a Valsalva LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg and an LVEF ≥55%. Week 8 represented the
start of the predefined maintenance phase during which subjects with oHCM received
echocardiographic assessments every 4 weeks through the end of trial at Week 24. Starting from
Week 8, the dose was down-titrated if the LVEF was <50%. If LVEF was <50% at any time, the
subject was returned to the prior dose level, or placed on placebo if the subject was on 5 mg.
Dosing titration was implemented using an interactive Wed Response System (IWRS) in CY
6031. A sonographer and cardiologist (both blinded to randomized treatment assignment)
performed and interpreted the echocardiogram and entered the results into the IWRS for dose
titrations and adjustments. Investigational product (IP) was dispensed at scheduled trial visits
using IWRS. Therefore, the investigator and site staff remained blinded to the echocardiogram
images and results as well as treatment group allocation and dosing.

Proposed To-Be-Marketed Dose Titration Scheme and Echocardiographic Assessment
Frequency
The goal of the proposed to-be-marketed aficamten daily dosing regimen is to maintain the
favorable benefit-risk profile established in the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031), while allowing for more
flexible clinic visits for subjects with oHCM. Therefore, the Applicant proposed visits of every
two to eight weeks during the dose titration phase and every three to six months during the
maintenance phase for echocardiographic assessments based on additional modeling and
simulations (See Section 14.5).
Table 10 provides a tabulated version of the proposed dosing algorithm. The echocardiographybased dose titration criteria (Rows 1 and 3 to 5) are identical to that employed in the Phase 3 trial
(Table 9) with the exception of the recommendation for subjects on the highest aficamten dose
(i.e., 20 mg) who still demonstrate LVEF ≥55% and LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg to move to the
maintenance phase (Table 10, Row 2). Proposed guidance on the resumption of aficamten
therapy in subjects with oHCM after dose interruption due to an LVEF <50% is also provided
(Table 10, Rows 6 to 8).

2. Assessment during maintenance phase: In the Phase 3 trial, echocardiographic assessments
were performed every 4 weeks during the maintenance phase regardless of LVEF. For the tobe-marketed regimen, the Applicant proposed echocardiographic assessments every 3 to 6
months for subjects in the maintenance phase, and to increase the frequency of
echocardiographic assessments to every 3 months for LVEF <55% to ≥50%. The Applicant’s
proposal of assessing LVEF and Valsalva LVOT-G every 3 to 6 months in subjects during
the maintenance phase appears reasonable. Figure 3 shows that the plasma levels of
aficamten remained stable over 6 months with low inter- and intra-subject variability (%CV:
33.9% and 20.5% respectively) during the maintenance phase of the Phase 3 trial, CY 6031,
with seven subjects having LVEF <50%. Thus, PK variability over time is modest and
contributes to the low within-subject variability of LVEF in the maintenance phase.
Considering there were seven subjects showing LVEF <50%, correlation analyses were
performed to identify the predictor (aficamten peak concentration or prior LVEF) for LVEF
<50% using the data from Trial CY 6031. The results suggested that prior LVEF has better
association with subsequent LVEF as compared to peak plasma concentration during the
maintenance phase (Figure 4). The risk of LVEF <50% was modestly higher (around 50%) in
Trial CY 6031 if the preceding LVEF was less than 55%. Considering LVEF <55% as a
predictor of subsequent LVEF <50%, correlation analyses were performed between LVEF at
Week 24 (LVEFwk24) and preceding LVEFs at various timepoints including Week 20 (4week preceding LVEFwk24), Week 16 (8-week preceding LVEFwk24), Week 12 (12-week
preceding LVEFwk24), and Week 8 (16-week preceding LVEFwk24) using Trial CY 6031 data
to identify the optimal assessment time for subjects with LVEF <55% to ≥50% (Figure 5).
The results suggested similar associations between prior LVEFs (i.e., 4-week preceding

LVEFwk24, 8-week preceding LVEFwk24, and 12-week preceding LVEFwk24) and subsequent

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM)
The primary assessment of efficacy of aficamten for the treatment of adults with symptomatic
oHCM was based on the results of the pivotal Phase 3 Trial CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM).
6.2.1.1. Study Design, CY 6031
SEQUOIA-HCM (CY 6031) was a multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled
trial in subjects with symptomatic oHCM. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to
receive aficamten or placebo in addition to background oHCM therapy for 24 weeks.
Randomization was stratified by use of beta-blockers (capped at 70% for beta-blocker users) and
CPET exercise modality (treadmill or bicycle). Other enrollment limits included disopyramide
users that were capped at 10% of total enrollment and subjects with persistent atrial fibrillation
(AF) at screening that were capped at 15% of total enrollment. Patients treated with
31
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
disopyramide represent a small and specific subgroup of oHCM patients who are symptomatic
despite standard of care. The pre-specified enrollment caps minimize the potential confounding
factors due to potential differences in patient characteristics and ensure generalizability of the
trial results.
Investigational product was administered orally once daily with or without food. The starting
dose of aficamten was 5 mg. Doses were individually titrated at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 based on
echocardiography-guided criteria (Table 12). Dose escalation with 5-mg increment at Weeks 2,
4, and 6 occurred only if a subject had a Valsalva LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg and LVEF ≥55%
(available doses: 5 mg, 10 mg 15 mg, and 20 mg). At any visit, the dose was down-titrated if the
LVEF was <50% (40% to 49%). If a subject’s LVEF was <40% at any time, IP would be
interrupted and held for at least 7 days. Repeat echocardiograms would be performed, and a
subject could be restarted on IP after down-titration when a normal LVEF (≥55%) was
documented.
Table 12. Echocardiogram Criteria for Scheduled Dose Titrations, Trial CY 6031

Source: CY 6031 Clinical Study Report, Table 2

The double-blind placebo-controlled treatment period lasted 24 weeks. Following the last dose of
IP, there was a 4-week safety follow-up period.

6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, CY 6031
Key Inclusion Criteria


Between 18 and 85 years old at screening.



Body mass index <35 kg/m2 at screening.



Acoustic windows to enable accurate transthoracic echocardiograms.



Diagnosed with HCM per the following criteria:
– Had LV hypertrophy with nondilated LV chambers in the absence of other cardiac causes
– Had an end-diastolic LV wall thickness ≥15 mm (or ≥13 mm with a known diseasecausing gene mutation or positive family history of HCM) as determined by core
laboratory interpretation, and
– Had resting LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg and Valsalva LVOT-G ≥50 mm Hg at screening.



LVEF ≥60% at screening as determined by the echocardiography core laboratory.



NYHA Functional Class II or III at screening.



Hemoglobin ≥10 g/dL at screening.



Respiratory exchange ratio ≥1.05 and pVO2≤90% at screening.



Subjects on beta-blockers, verapamil, diltiazem, or disopyramide should have been on a
stable regimen for >6 weeks prior to randomization and anticipate remaining on the same
medication regimen during the trial. Subjects treated with disopyramide must also be treated
concomitantly with a beta blocker and/or calcium channel blocker.

Key Exclusion Criteria


Significant valvular heart disease:
– Moderate-severe valvular aortic stenosis and/or regurgitation.
– Moderate-severe mitral regurgitation not due to systolic anterior motion of the mitral
valve.



Documented history of current obstructive coronary artery disease or myocardial infarction.



Known or suspected infiltrative, genetic or storage disorder causing cardiac hypertrophy that
mimics oHCM.



Prior treatment with cardiotoxic agents such as doxorubicin.



History of LV systolic dysfunction (LVEF <45%) or stress cardiomyopathy at any time
during their clinical course.



Had any electrocardiogram abnormality considered by the investigator to pose a risk to
subject safety (e.g., second degree atrioventricular block type II).



Documented paroxysmal AF during the screening period.

Paroxysmal or permanent AF is only excluded if:
– Rhythm restoring treatment has been required ≤6 months prior to screening.
– Rate control and anticoagulation has not been achieved at least 6 months prior to
screening.



History of syncope or sustained ventricular tachyarrhythmia with exercise within 6 months
prior to screening.



Implantable cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD) placement within 3 months prior to screening or

6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, CY 6031

The review division provided comments on the Trial CY 6031 protocol (preliminary meeting
minutes dated June 1, 2021, End-of-Phase 2 meeting minutes dated August 3, 2021) and the
statistical analysis plan (SAP) (dated August 11, 2023, and November 1, 2023).
Sample Size
The Applicant specified a sample size of 270 subjects, randomized 1:1 to aficamten or placebo,
providing >90% power to detect a change from baseline in pVO2 to Week 24 of 1.5 mL/kg/min
compared to placebo (2-sided type I error of 0.05), assuming a standard deviation of
3.5 mL/kg/min, and controlling for limiting beta-blocker use (less than ~70%), limiting exercise
modality of bicycle (less than ~50%), and 10% of subjects with missing data (i.e., change from
baseline data of the primary endpoint).
During the trial, the Applicant pre-specified periodic assessments of the blinded aggregate
pooled missing data rate and overall pooled SD for the change from baseline in pVO2 at Week
24. Based on these assessments, the Applicant could increase the sample size once to maintain
the intended power. Thus, there were 282 subjects, rather than 270, enrolled and randomized in
the trial.
Efficacy Endpoints
Primary
The primary endpoint of the trial was Change in pVO2 by CPET from baseline to Week 24.

34
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Key Secondary


Change in KCCQ-CSS from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24.



Proportion of subjects with ≥1 class improvement in NYHA Functional Class from baseline
to Week 12 and Week 24.



Change in Valsalva LVOT-G from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24.



Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 12 and Week 24.



Total duration of SRT eligibility during the 24-week treatment period in subjects who were
SRT eligible at baseline.



Change in total workload during CPET from baseline to Week 24.

Primary Analysis Population
Primary efficacy analyses were performed on the full analysis set (FAS), which included all
randomized subjects.
Efficacy Analyses
Efficacy analyses were performed in the FAS by the randomized treatment group. All hypothesis
tests were reported as 2-sided p-values. To preserve an overall type I error rate of 0.05, testing of
the primary and secondary endpoints followed a specified order using a closed testing procedure
as described below.
Null and Alternative Hypotheses
The null hypothesis for the primary endpoint was that the treatment difference (aficamten minus
placebo) of the mean change from baseline of pVO2 at Week 24 was 0.
Multiple Comparisons and Multiplicity
A closed testing procedure was used to address multiplicity and preserve the overall type I error
rate. The primary endpoint was tested first at a two-sided 0.05 alpha threshold. If the primary
endpoint achieved statistical significance, secondary endpoints were then tested in sequential
order at the two-sided 0.05 level:


KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 24.



Proportion of subjects with ≥1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 24.



Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 24.



Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 24.



Duration of SRT eligibility up to 24 weeks of treatment for those who were SRT eligible at
baseline.



KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 12.



Proportion of subjects with ≥1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 12.



Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 12.
35

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)


Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 12.



Change from baseline in total workload at Week 24.

Primary Endpoint Analysis
The primary analysis was performed using an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model that
included terms of treatment, randomization stratification factors (beta-blocker use status and
CPET modality), baseline pVO2 and baseline body weight as covariates in the FAS. Data
analyzed include all observed Week 24 pVO2 values from subjects who completed 24 weeks of
treatment, as well as from subjects who early terminate from the treatment but remain in the trial
with Week 24 pVO2 measurements. Data were imputed for subjects who were missing Week 24
pVO2. Missing data were imputed using multiple imputation methodology under the missing at
random (MAR) assumption for the primary analysis. One hundred imputed MAR datasets were
generated. Results were combined using Rubin’s rule to produce an LS mean estimate of the
treatment difference, its 95% confidence interval (CI), and p-value (p). Additional statistical
details are provided in Section 16).
Secondary Endpoints Analysis
Change in KCCQ-CSS and change in Valsalva LVOT-G from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24
were analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) model with baseline as a
covariate, randomization stratification factors, visit, treatment group, and interaction terms of
treatment by visit and baseline by visit. Since there was a >10% difference in baseline KCCQCSS across regions, treatment by visit and region and baseline by visit and region were also
included in the model. The treatment effect at each visit week was estimated from the interaction
terms of treatment by visit by region, with coefficients for each region determined by proportion
of subjects evaluated in each region. An unstructured covariance matrix was specified. All data
observed up to Week 24 post-randomization was included in the model. The LS mean estimates,
differences, 95% CIs, and p-values were reported for the results.
The proportion of subjects with ≥1 class improvement in NYHA Functional Class and proportion
of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G <30 mm Hg at Week 12 and Week 24 were analyzed using
the Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel test stratified by randomization factors. For the proportion of
subjects with ≥1 class improvement in NYHA class at Week 12, Week 16 NYHA class was used
if Week 12 NYHA class was not available. Week 8 NYHA class was used if Week 16 NYHA
was not available essentially making this a closest observation imputation. Subject was
considered as not achieving ≥1 improvement in NYHA class at Week 12 if no NYHA at Weeks
8, 12, and 16 was available. Similarly, Week 20 NYHA class was used if Week 24 NYHA was
not available. Subjects were considered as not achieving ≥1 improvement in NYHA class at
Week 24 if Week 20 and Week 24 NYHA were not available.
For the proportion of subjects with post-Valsalva LVOT <30 mm Hg at Week 12, Week 16 postValsalva LVOT was used if the Week 12 visit was performed but post-Valsalva LVOT was not
available. Week 8 post-Valsalva LVOT was used if Week 16 was not available. Similarly, Week
20 post Valsalva LVOT was used if Week 24 was performed but post Valsalva LVOT was not
available. The p-value and 95% CI were obtained using an exact method.
Total duration of SRT eligibility was calculated as the number of days subjects are SRT eligible,
from the date of the randomization until Week 24. Because the SRT eligibility status of a subject
36
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
may change during the trial conduct, only intervals during which the subject is SRT eligible,
were summed. Total duration of SRT eligibility during the 24-week of treatment period was
analyzed using an ANCOVA model that includes treatment and randomization stratification
factor beta blocker use/nonuse as fixed effects, and significant baseline characteristics as
covariates. Significance of baseline covariates was evaluated using a stepwise model selection
method with a default stay or entry level of 0.05.
Change in total workload during CPET from baseline to Week 24 was analyzed using the same
analysis approach as for the primary endpoint analysis.
It should be noted that the review team did not agree with the Applicant’s approach for missing
data. For the primary analyses, the Applicant imputed the missing data under the missing-atrandom assumption despite the advice provided by the biometrics division, dated August 11,
2023, on the CY 6031 statistical analysis plan (SAP):
We continue to disagree that an MAR assumption is appropriate given that patients with
missing data are likely to be different from those who remain in the trial on treatment. It
is generally not acceptable because patients who drop out of the study and are no longer
on study treatment are not expected to behave in the same way as those who continue in
the study on treatment, which would not be in line with a treatment policy estimand, and
those who die should not be imputed with similar outcomes to those who are able to
remain in the study. You can use different imputation models based on the type of
intercurrent events which are the reasons for missing data. For example, retrieved
dropouts (those who discontinue treatment but continue study visits) can be used to
impute data for those who dropout of the study. For those who die before the primary
endpoint can be assessed, we generally accept a worst-case type of imputation where the
worst 10% of observed outcomes can be used for a multiple imputation of missing data
due to death.

Additionally, the review team did not agree with the use of stepwise model selection approach
due to well-known issues with overestimation of model fit and increase in type I error. In this
case, no adjusting covariates were ultimately included in the model.

The remaining screened subjects (n=282) were enrolled and randomized to treatment (142 to

subject withdrawal, two due to a protocol deviation, and one due to an adverse event (AE). Of
the subjects randomized to placebo, four (2.9%) discontinued treatment early: two due to an AE,
one due to physician decision, and one due to other reason. All subjects in both treatment groups
received at least one dose of IP, so the All-Randomized set matched the primary FAS.

Baseline Characteristics
Table 17 examines the between-group balance on baseline clinical characteristics in the FAS
population. At baseline, subjects had a median body mass index of 28.1 kg/m2. Most subjects
had NYHA Class II heart failure (75.9%). The mean LVEF was 74.8%, the mean resting LVOTG was 55.1 mm Hg, and the mean Valsalva LVOT-G was 83.1 mm Hg. A total of 61 subjects
(32 aficamten, 29 placebo) were SRT eligible at baseline. The mean pVO2 by CPET at baseline
was 18.5 mL/kg/min, with 55% of subjects using treadmill as the CPET modality. The baseline
clinical characteristics were generally balanced between the aficamten and placebo treatment
groups.

The statistical reviewer performed the pre-specified analyses to verify the Applicant’s results and
performed additional analyses where missing data were imputed under missing not at random
assumptions (MNAR) using placebo-based multiple imputation.

Because FDA did not agree to the MAR assumption made in the primary analysis, the review
team conducted an additional analysis imputing missing data from subjects who discontinued
42
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
aficamten using placebo-based multiple imputation. There were not a sufficient number of

who remained on aficamten treatment were imputed assuming MAR based on the model derived
from observed data within the aficamten group. Results from this analysis, 1.69 mL/kg/min (95%

Sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint are presented in Section 16. Results are generally
consistent with the primary analysis.
Primary Endpoint Subgroup Analysis Results
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect
for various demographic groups and are presented in Section 16. Overall, the treatment effect of
aficamten when compared to placebo was generally consistent with the overall population across
demographic subgroups of age, sex , race, and ethnicity. Note that ethnic and racial minorities
were not well-represented in this trial.
Secondary Endpoints Analyses Results
All secondary endpoints were found to be statistically significant for both the Applicant’s
specified methodology, as well as the additional FDA analyses (Table 20, Table 21, Table 22,
Table 23, Table 24, and Table 25).
KCCQ-CSS
The mean baseline KCCQ-CSS score was approximately 75 (scores range from 0-100, with
higher scores indicating better health status). The estimated KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at
Week 24 was 11.6 points for aficamten and 4.3 points for placebo with an estimated LS mean
difference of 7.3 points (95% CI: 4.6, 10.1; p<0.0001). For change from baseline at Week 12,
estimates were 11.1 points for aficamten and 4.0 points for placebo. The LS mean difference was
7.0 points (95% CI: 4.5, 9.5; p<0.0001). Seven subjects had missing KCCQ-CSS (4 in aficamten
and 3 in placebo) data at Week 24. Missing data were handled as MAR by the MMRM model in
the prespecified analysis.
The statistical reviewer performed an additional analysis where intermittent missing data were
imputed using the Markov Chain Monte Carlo method under MAR assumption. After the first
step of imputation, the monotone missing data were imputed using placebo-based imputations.
Imputed datasets were analyzed using an MMRM model. The LS mean difference between
treatment groups at Week 12 was 7.0 points (95% CI: 4.6, 9.5; p<0.0001) and at Week 24 was
7.3 points (95% CI: 4.2, 10.0; p<0.0001), essentially unchanged from the primary analysis
method.

An information request was submitted on December 18, 2024, and December 23, 2024,
regarding the clinical meaningfulness of the KCCQ-CSS instrument and datasets with variables
indicating the values imputed for missing data. The Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) team
was consulted to assess clinical meaningfulness of the KCCQ-CSS results and the proposed
labeling claim (COA review dated October 10, 2025). The COA team concluded that KCCQCSS was fit-for-purpose to support its context of use in subjects with oHCM; and the
Applicant's proposed threshold of 10 points as a reflection of KCCQ-CSS within-subject
clinically meaningful change from baseline to Week 24 appeared reasonable. The COA team
further concluded that the evidence provided by the Applicant generally supported the
proposed labeling claims.
NYHA Improvement
There was a statistically significantly greater proportion of aficamten-treated subjects with a ≥1
class improvement in NYHA functional class compared with placebo-treated subjects at Week
44
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
12: 48.6% versus 17.9% with an odds ratio of 4.6 (95% CI: 2.6, 8.5; p<0.0001). Similarly, at
Week 24, a statistically significantly greater proportion of aficamten-treated subjects had a ≥1
class improvement in NYHA functional class compared with placebo-treated subjects: 58.5%
versus 24.3% with an odds ratio of 4.4 (95% CI: 2.6, 7.6; p<0.0001). There were 6 (2.1%)
subjects at Week 24 and 4 (1.4%) subjects at Week 12 for whom NYHA class value was not

The statistical reviewer performed additional analysis where all subjects with missing data were

Duration of SRT Eligibility
SRT eligibility was defined as a subject being classified as NYHA class ≥3 and with resting or

−78.1 days (95% CI: −99.8, −56.3; p<0.0001) compared with placebo. There were no missing

6.2.2. CY 6021 (REDWOOD-HCM)
The REDWOOD-HCM study was a Phase 2, multi-center, randomized, placebo-controlled,
dose-finding study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics
of aficamten in adults with symptomatic HCM. The study consisted of 4 cohorts with treatment
duration of 10 weeks with a 4-week follow-up period after the last dose. Cohort 1 consisted of
subjects with oHCM not receiving disopyramide who were randomized 2:1 (n=14; n=8) to
receive up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (5 mg, 10 mg, and 15 mg once daily) or placebo.
Cohort 2 (n=14 aficamten, n=6 placebo) was a replica of Cohort 1 except for the 3 escalating
doses of aficamten (10 mg, 20 mg, 30 mg once daily). Cohort 3 consisted of subjects with oHCM
whose background HCM therapy included disopyramide. All subjects in Cohort 3 (n=13)
received up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (5, 10, and 15 mg) based on echocardiographic
guidance. Cohort 4 consisted of subjects (n=41) with nHCM whose background therapy included
beta-blockers and/or calcium channel blockers, either as monotherapy or in combination therapy.
Subjects receiving disopyramide were excluded from Cohort 4. Subjects in Cohort 4 received up
to 3 doses of aficamten (5, 10, and 15 mg).

49
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Efficacy was evaluated by LVOT-G, NYHA classification, and subject-reported outcome quality
of life assessment. There were no adjustments for multiplicity. For subjects in Cohort 1 and 2,
there were statistically significant reductions in LVOT-G, both resting and post-Valsalva, from
baseline compared to pooled placebo at Week 10:


Cohort 1:
– Resting LVOT-G: -41 mm Hg aficamten; -15 mm Hg placebo (nominal p=0.0009)
– Post-Valsalva LVOT-G: -40 mm Hg aficamten; -5 mm Hg placebo (nominal p=0.0005)



Cohort 2:
– Resting LVOT-G: -40 mm Hg aficamten; -15 mm Hg pooled placebo (nominal p=0.001)
– Post-Valsalva LVOT-G: -51 mm Hg aficamten; -5 mm Hg pooled placebo (nominal
p<0.0001)

Improvements of at least one NYHA functional class from baseline to Week 10 were observed in
43% and 64% of subjects treated with aficamten in Cohorts 1 and 2, respectively, compared to
33% of the pooled placebo-treated subjects (see Section 16.2).
Cohorts 3 and 4 did not have respective control groups. Further, subjects in Cohort 4 had
nonobstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy for which an LVOT-G evaluation was not possible.
Thus, data interpretation was challenging in Cohorts 3 and 4 and, therefore, not considered to be
confirmatory evidence.
In summary, the REDWOOD-HCM study demonstrated sizeable PD effects on LVOT-G when
added to standard-of-care. These findings reasonably confirm/support the results from the pivotal
SEQUOIA-HCM trial.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
6.3.1. Is the Observed Treatment Effect of Aficamten
on pVO2 Clinically Meaningful?
Issue
Baseline pVO2 was approximately 18 mL/kg/min. The primary endpoint of change in pVO2 from
baseline to Week 24 was statistically significant (p<0.0001) with a mean difference of
1.7 mL/kg/min between treatment groups. The clinical meaningfulness of the result needs to be
established.
Background
In HCM, pVO2 is associated with clinical prognosis and death (Masri et al. 2015); however, the
relationship between a change in pVO2 and clinical outcomes is not extensively studied or well
defined. An increase of 1 mL/kg/min in pVO2 was found to be predictive of reduced risk of death
from HF and need for heart transplantation in patients with HCM (Coats et al. 2015). However,
this study was a retrospective observational study from a single center, and the generalizability
of the study findings is uncertain. In patients with HF and reduced ejection fraction, a small
increase in pVO2 (~1 mL/kg/min) was associated with a 5% lower risk of all-cause mortality or
50
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
all-cause hospitalization (p<0.001), a 4% lower risk of cardiovascular mortality or cardiovascular
hospitalization, and a 7% lower risk of all-cause mortality in a randomized-controlled trial
(Swank et al. 2012). However, it is uncertain whether the described association of pVO2 with
cardiovascular outcomes in patients with HF with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) with LVEF
≤35% can be extrapolated to the HCM population with preserved LV systolic function and
patients who have a reasonably normal life expectancy overall. Current evidence is insufficient
to quantify the magnitude of clinical benefit associated with a small increase in pVO2 in with
HCM. Despite the uncertainties, it should be noted that improvement with aficamten treatment
was observed across a broader range of clinical endpoints including statistical and clinical
significance in KCCQ-CSS and NYHA Class. Other supporting efficacy results include large,
statistically significant findings in LVOT-G reduction and improvement in total workload from
the CPET. The observed consistent results clearly establish that aficamten improves how patients
with oHCM function and feel. Additional analyses to demonstrate the clinical significance of the
observed pVO2 changes are described below.
Assessment
Clinical Outcomes Assessment Consult Review
The Applicant did not propose a range of pVO2 change that is clinically meaningful to patients.
COA was consulted during the NDA review for assistance in interpreting meaningful change in
pVO2 scores. Exploratory anchor-based analyses using NYHA, KCCQ-Physical Limitation
Score, and Patient Global Impressions of Change have been conducted. However, given the low
correlation coefficients (i.e., |r| <0.3) between pVO2 and the anchor scales for the changes from
baseline at Week 24, a suitable anchor scale to estimate pVO2 meaningful change in CY 6031
was not identified.
While the threshold for within-subject clinically meaningful change from baseline at Week 24 in
pVO2 could not be estimated, the cumulative distribution function curves show a clear separation
between groups across a wide range of pVO2 improvement change scores in CY 6031.
Figure 7. Cumulative Distribution of Change From Baseline to Week 24 in pVO2, Full Analysis Set,
Trial CY 6031

Source: CY 6031 CSR, Figure 14.2.3
Abbreviation: pVO2, peak oxygen consumption

functional improvement defined as:




A pVO2 increase of ≥3.0 mL/kg/min and no worsening of NYHA class at Week 24

A greater proportion of subjects in the aficamten group achieved this composite efficacy
endpoint compared to the placebo group (42.3% versus 13.6%, respectively) (see Table 26).

The division considers that a change in NYHA Class of 1 or more represents a clinically
meaningful improvement. Adding NYHA class as a component of the efficacy endpoint
strengths the validity of clinical response. It is also important to note that some subjects can have
more sizeable improvements in pVO2, as evidenced for example, by the proportion of subjects
with ≥3.0 mL/kg/min improvement without worsening in NYHA class. Approximately 26% of
aficamten-treated subjects met this component of the exploratory efficacy endpoint compared to
9% of subjects treated with placebo. Furthermore, this composite endpoint was previously used
as a primary efficacy endpoint to support approval of mavacamten, the first-in-class, cardiac
myosin inhibitor (NDA 214998) in the phase 3 EXPLORER-HCM trial. The division considered
the improvement defined in this composite endpoint clinical meaningful. The proportion of
subjects who achieved this composite endpoint in EXPLORER-HCM was 37% versus 17% in
the mavacamten and placebo group, respectively with a difference of 19.4% and odds ratio of 2.7
(p =0.0005). Change in pVO2 was also studied as a secondary endpoint in EXPLORER-HCM
52
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
with a mean difference of 1.4 mL/kg/min. Overall, the aficamten treatment effects on the
exploratory composite endpoint of pVO2 and NYHA functional class, as well as on the
continuous pVO2, were similar to those observed with mavacamten.
Conclusion
The totality of data indicates that the observed changes in pVO2 in CY 6031 are both statistically
and clinically significant.

6.3.2. Impact of Imputation of Missing Data for
Continuous Variable Endpoints on Assessment of
Efficacy
Issue
For the primary analyses of the primary and continuous secondary endpoints, the Applicant
imputed all the missing data using multiple imputations under missing at random assumption
regardless of reasons for the missing data.
Background
The source of missing data, particularly when linked to intercurrent events, plays a key role in
determining how missing data should be handled. While missingness due to external, studyindependent factors may be classified as MAR, this is uncommon. In most cases, missing data
are associated with treatment adherence. It is generally not appropriate to impute missing data
from non-adherent subjects using data from adherent subjects within the same treatment group,
as differences in treatment exposure and behavior can lead to biased estimates of the treatment
effect. We recommend that missing data from non-adherent subjects in the primary analyses be
addressed using data from other non-adherent subjects within the same group who have available
measurements at the specified analysis timepoint (retrieved dropouts). See Section 6.2.1.3.
Assessment
The statistical reviewer performed additional analyses where missing data for subjects who
discontinued the treatment were imputed as missing not at random using placebo-based multiple
imputation. There were not enough data to perform a retrieved dropouts’ imputation. The results
of these analyses are shown in Table 18, Table 20, Table 22, and Table 25.
Conclusion
The results of the additional analyses were generally similar to those seen in the Applicant’s
analyses and did not alter overall conclusions regarding efficacy. Results from the appropriate
analyses that are generally recommended and implemented for these endpoints should be used
for labeling purposes.

53
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

6.3.3. Impact of Imputation of Missing Data for
Categorical Variable Endpoints on Assessment of
Efficacy
Issue
For the categorical secondary endpoints, the Applicant imputed missing data using single
imputation using measurements from other weeks; either last observation carried forward or next
observation carried backward.
Background
The review division does not typically accept single imputation methods as this ignores
uncertainty which will underestimate variability. Multiple imputation, such as a retrieved
dropouts or jump-to reference, are typically preferred. Also, we do not agree with last
observation carried forward or next observation carried backward imputation as it may introduce
bias, misrepresent treatment effect, and underestimate variability by assuming that outcome
remains constant over time. Although failure imputation is a single imputation method for
responder analyses, such an analysis could be acceptable if the imputation was accounted for in
the interpretation of the results with little missing data that is similar between groups (i.e., one
group was not proportionally higher in missing data rates).
Assessment
The statistical reviewer performed additional analyses as shown in Table 21 and Table 23. For
the NYHA functional class categorical endpoint, subjects with missing data were classified as
non-responders in the analysis. For the categorical Valsalva LVOT-G endpoint (Table 23),
missing values imputed for the analysis of the continuous Valsalva LVOT-G were categorized as
responders and non-responders based on the imputed values prior to the analysis.
Conclusion
The results were generally similar to those seen in the Applicant’s analyses and did not alter
overall conclusions regarding efficacy. Results from the appropriate analyses that are generally
recommended and implemented for these endpoints should be used for labeling purposes.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
All toxicology species were pharmacologically relevant considering the evolutionary
conservation of cardiac myosin across mammals. A brief discussion of clinically relevant
toxicities is included below. Detailed summaries of the nonclinical studies are provided in
Section 13.1.

54
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Exaggerated Pharmacology/Cardiac Toxicity
Cardiac effects were, unsurprisingly, observed in repeat-dose toxicology studies conducted in
rats and dogs at or near exposure levels anticipated for clinical use, which were interpreted as
direct results of the intended pharmacology in healthy animals. A similar pattern was observed in
the toxicology studies with mavacamten, the first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor.
In healthy rats, the cardiac findings, which were interpreted as overpredicting clinical risk,
consisted of dose-dependent increases in irreversible dilated cardiomyopathy, increased heart
weight, ventricular wall thinning, and premature deaths at exposures ranging from approximately
4-and 11-times the clinical exposures based on free AUC and free Cmax, respectively, at the
maximum recommended human dose (MRHD). Continued treatment of healthy rats culminated
in heart failure and deaths in a dose and duration-dependent fashion.
In shorter studies in healthy dogs, at exposures exceeding the anticipated clinical exposures at
the MRHD, aficamten caused dose-dependent cardiac effects including increased heart rate,
ventricular dilatation, increased heart weight, and cardiomyocyte degeneration with necrosis,
with high-dose animals showing persistent ventricular dilatation and increased heart weight
(males only) even after a recovery period. In a longer 39-week study at lower doses (up to 6times clinical exposure), aficamten was better tolerated with only reversible increases in heart
rate and QTc interval, and transient heart weight increases in males that resolved by study
termination.
Effects Secondary to Cardiac Toxicity
Inhibiting cardiac contractility in otherwise healthy animals led to secondary effects on various
organ systems due to altered hemodynamics and compromised blood perfusion. These effects
were particularly notable in the liver and the lung. Acute liver necrosis with elevated hepatic
enzymes occurred in high-dose male rats secondary to congestive heart failure caused by
aficamten-induced reduced cardiac contractility, with no hepatotoxicity observed at lower doses
in rats or in dog studies. Pulmonary effects in rats included increased lung weight with
hemosiderin-laden alveolar macrophages and lung hemorrhage in high-dose males, consistent
with secondary lung edema due to heart failure associated with cardiac dilatation. Taken
together, no unanticipated off-target toxicities were observed in healthy animals with repeat
aficamten dosing. The clinical relevance of the findings was considered limited for the target
patient population.
Mutagenicity/Carcinogenicity
Aficamten was not mutagenic or clastogenic in a standard battery of genotoxicity studies
including the Ames assay and a combined in vivo micronucleus assay and comet assay.
There were no aficamten-related tumor findings in a 26-week carcinogenicity study in transgenic
rasH2 mice at doses up to 2.0 mg/kg/day or in a 2-year carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley
rats at doses up to 3 mg/kg/day (corresponding to 4-times the exposure at the MRHD based on
AUCunbound).
Developmental Toxicity
In embryofetal and pre- and postnatal development studies, when pregnant rats were
administered aficamten during the period of organogenesis, aficamten increased the incidence of
55
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
structural malformations at exposures ≥4-times the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 20 mg
based on AUCunbound. No adverse effects on development were seen at 3-times the clinical
exposure at the MRHD. See Sections 8.4 and 13.1 for more details.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Aficamten is a cardiac myosin inhibitor. The primary risk associated with this class of drugs is
the potential for development of signs or symptoms of HF due to an exaggerated pharmacologic
effect resulting in systolic dysfunction (reduced LVEF). The risk is greater among subjects who
concurrently take medications that inhibit multiple P450 pathways involved in aficamten
elimination.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Aficamten is not approved in the United States or in any foreign market.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
FDA’s safety evaluation focused on the data obtained in CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM). This
pivotal study provides controlled data to evaluate the safety profile of aficamten under
echocardiography-based dosing and monitoring in oHCM subjects. As agreed, the Applicant
provided a Summary of Clinical Safety that included safety data from the CY 6031, CY 6021
(dose-ranging, placebo-controlled Phase 2 study) and CY 6022 (ongoing open-label extension
study) (see the overview of clinical trials in Table 3). Given the different dose groups and dosing
strategies between studies, pooled controlled data from CY 6031 and CY 6021 were not
performed. Data from CY 6021 were evaluated separately as supportive safety data. The ongoing
extension Study CY 6022 provides long-term safety data in oHCM to evaluate safety topics of
interest.
The overall data and submission quality are reasonable. Adverse events were analyzed by use of
the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA; Version 27.0) preferred term, and
by pooling AEs with a similar medical concept (referred to as the Standardised MedDRA Query
or Office of New Drugs Custom Medical Queries [OCMQs]). All AEs presented and discussed
are treatment-emergent AEs unless specified otherwise. Treatment-emergent AEs are defined as
any AE with an onset date on or after the first dose of study treatment, or with worsening on or
after the first dose of study treatment up through 28 days after the last dose of study treatment
(aficamten has a half-life of ~3.5 days).
FDA’s safety review focused on categorizing the on-target effects of aficamten on decreasing
ejection fraction and increasing the risk of systolic cardiac failure. Clinical events of special
interest included major adverse cardiac events, new-onset persistent AF and ventricular
arrhythmias. Other safety evaluations included the analyses of exposure, AEs, echocardiographic
data, laboratory data and vital signs.

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

weeks among 296 subjects with oHCM. A summary of exposure in CY 6031 and CY 6022 is
shown in Table 27 and Table 28, respectively. The size of the safety database and duration of
exposure are acceptable given the prevalence of the disease.

leading to treatment interruption in the aficamten group (same number in the placebo group)
were cholecystitis and COVID-19; both events were transient and resolved, allowing the subjects
to complete the treatment.
