7.6.1.2. Deaths
No deaths occurred in either treatment group in CY 6031. With the updated safety data through
August 31, 2024, there were also no deaths reported in the ongoing extension Study CY 6022.
One death due to cardiac arrest occurred in the dose-ranging Study CY 6021. This death
involved a patient with nHCM and was deemed unlikely to be related to aficamten. A summary
of this case is provided below:

58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
(b) (6) : A 43-year-old Asian female subject with symptomatic nHCM
Subject CY-6021received escalating doses of aficamten (5 mg, 10 mg, and 15 mg) over 45 days. On Day 45, the
subject suffered a cardiac arrest while showering and died despite resuscitation efforts. The
subject was receiving aficamten 15 mg. The investigator assessed the cardiac arrest as severe but
not related to the study drug. This assessment was based on several factors: the subject's LVEF
remained within the normal range throughout the study (73% at baseline and 75% at the Week 6
visit and during hospital admission on Day 45), global longitudinal strain improved, NT-proBNP
and Troponin I levels improved compared to baseline. Additionally, the subject had a history of
aborted sudden cardiac death, which was considered a likely cause of the cardiac arrest.

7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Overall, the incidence of SAEs was similar between groups in CY 6031. No subject in the
aficamten group discontinued treatment due to an SAE. No SAE was considered by the
investigator to be related to study treatment. Narrative review of the cases in the aficamten group
did not reveal major concerns. The three SAEs of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in the aficamten
group occurred after discontinuation of treatment during the safety follow-up period (range from
8-22 days after the last aficamten treatment).

7.6.1.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Hypertension was the most common AE reported in CY 6031. It was also the only AE that
occurred at a frequency of ≥5% higher in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group
(8% versus 2%) (Table 33). Evaluation of hypertension is discussed in Section 7.6.1.6.1.
Palpitations was another common AE reported in CY 6031, occurring more frequently in
subjects on aficamten than placebo (7% versus 3%). Palpitations are subjective symptoms that
are common in patients with HCM. These symptoms are nonspecific and can be attributed to a
wide range of causes and underlying diseases. The palpitation events observed in the aficamten
group were transient and did not recur despite continued treatment. There was no apparent
pattern in the onset of these events relative to the initiation of aficamten treatment (ranging from
4 to 360 days). Importantly, reports of palpitations were not associated with any clinically
significant events, such as arrhythmia. Given these observations with a low incidence, a causal
relationship between these palpitation events and aficamten treatment was unlikely.
61
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Heart failure related-events were reported in four aficamten-treated subjects (three cases reported
as cardiac failure and one case reported as pulmonary edema) and one placebo-treated subject
(cardiac failure congestive). Three of the events in the aficamten group occurred after
discontinuation of treatment, during the safety follow-up period (range from two to eight days
after last aficamten treatment). Given the long half-life of aficamten, a causal relationship
between aficamten and these events cannot be excluded. The single on-treatment AE of cardiac
failure (moderate intensity) was thought to be related to aficamten (see more details in
Section 7.6.1.7).
Numerical imbalance of back pain, paresthesia, renal and urinary tract infection, and rash was
also observed in CY 6031; however, the frequency of these AEs was overall low, and the
difference between groups was small (<3%). These events were transient and generally reported
to be mild in severity. Due to the nature of the events, the significance of these events in
aficamten-treated subjects is unclear and these minor imbalances are likely due to chance.

7.6.1.6.1. Hypertension
There was a small imbalance in hypertension AEs in Trial CY 6031; 11 (8%) on aficamten
compared to 3 (2%) on placebo. The majority of the hypertension events in the aficamten group
were mild (8 out of 11), with no severe cases or SAEs reported. None of the hypertension events
led to treatment discontinuation, interruption or dose changes. Most subjects who experienced
hypertension had reported a medical history of hypertension at baseline (8 out of 11). There was

62
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
no apparent pattern of event onset relative to aficamten treatment initiation (the time to onset
ranged from 15 to 173 days).
A total of 15 subjects (7%) experienced hypertension AEs in the open-label extension Study CY
6022. The nature and severity of these events were consistent with the results in CY 6031. There
was one SAE of hypertensive urgency that occurred in a 57-year-old female subject with oHCM
and a history of essential hypertension. Their baseline blood pressure (BP) was elevated at
174/92 mm Hg. The SAE occurred 13 days after initiating aficamten treatment. The subject
presented to the emergency room after experiencing significantly elevated BP of 214/99 mm Hg
at home and was subsequently hospitalized due to the SAE. Aficamten (5 mg) was interrupted,
and the event resolved after 2 days. The subject restarted aficamten and was subsequently uptitrated to 10 mg and 15 mg without any recurrence of the hypertensive event.
Consistent with the AE findings, there was a small mean increase in both systolic and diastolic
BP in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group (see Section 7.6.1.10). Aficamtenassociated increases in BP are thought to be a consequence of relief of LVOT obstruction with
improved cardiac output. The clinical review team recommends describing the hypertension AE
findings and blood pressure changes in Section 6 of the labeling.
7.6.1.7. Echocardiograms: LVEF
Consistent with the aficamten mechanism of action, there was a small mean decrease in LVEF
(~4 to 5% with an SD of 7%) during 24 weeks of aficamten treatment compared with placebo
(Figure 8). Mean LVEF returned toward baseline at Week 28, following the 4-week safety
follow-up period.
Figure 8. Mean LVEF Across Time, Safety Population, Trial CY 6031

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: SAS
Abbreviations: EOS, end of study; EOT, end of treatment; LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; SAS, statistical analysis software

63
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
LVEF values <50% as assessed by the core laboratory were observed for five subjects (4%) in
the aficamten group and one subject (1%) in the placebo group. The median LVEF among the
five subjects in the aficamten group was 47% (range from 34% to 49%). Of the five subjects in
the aficamten group, none had associated signs or symptoms of heart failure, and none had
LVEF <30%. There was only one subject who had concurrent LVEF <50% as assessed by both
core-laboratory and site-assessed LVEF; the site assessment led to dose reduction. For the
remaining 4 aficamten subjects, no dose adjustments were made because the site-assessed LVEF
remained >50%. Core laboratory-assessed LVEF values returned to >50% during treatment for
four subjects and by the end of the washout period for all five subjects.
Similarly, site-assessed LVEF <50% was observed for seven subjects (5%) in the aficamten
group and one subject (1%) in the placebo group. All 7 aficamten-treated subjects had a
subsequent dose reduction (per protocol), and all subjects had an LVEF ≥50% at the next visit.
There was no treatment interruption due to LVEF <50%. There was one aficamten-treated
subject with reported associated signs or symptoms of heart failure. This 41-year-old White male
subject reported a nonserious AE of cardiac failure with moderate intensity at Week 12 (4 weeks
before a site read LVEF of 47% at Week 16). The site-assessed LVEF at Week 12 was 55%,
which was decreased from 65% at Week 8 (66% at baseline). No action was taken with
aficamten due to the event. The investigator assessed this AE as related to study treatment. At
Week 16, aficamten was down-titrated from 20 mg to 15 mg due to site-assessed LVEF <50%.
About 10 days after down-titration, LVEF returned to ≥50% based on both site-read and core
laboratory. The AE of cardia failure was considered resolved.
There was no specific pattern regarding the onset of LVEF ≤50%, which occurred sporadically
during the entire treatment period (median onset of 97 days, range from 15 days to 169 days).
7.6.1.8. Laboratory Findings
Analyses of laboratory data from the CY 6031 trial did not raise major safety concerns. Mean
changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the groups for most clinical
chemistry and hematology parameters collected in the study. The outlier analyses did not reveal
signals of concern. There was one subject in the aficamten group who had elevated creatine
phosphokinase (CPK) (>10× upper limit of normal [ULN]). The considerably high level of CPK
was observed during the safety follow-up period about 20 days after the last aficamten treatment.
The elevated CPK was transient and returned to normal after 2 weeks.

7.6.1.9. Assessment of Drug-Indced Liver Injury
Review of liver enzyme tests demonstrated that no subject met the criteria for drug-induced liver
injury assessed by concurrent alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase
(AST) >3x ULN and total bilirubin >2x ULN (i.e., possible Hy’s Law cases) in either treatment
group in CY 6031 (Figure 9). Four aficamten-treated subjects had ALT and/or AST >3x ULN
during the study period in Trial CY 6031(Figure 9 and Table 36). In all four cases, the
transaminase elevations were mild, transient, not associated with symptoms or increased
bilirubin, and did not require treatment interruption (see narratives in Section 17.1). One subject
with AST >5x ULN had an AST value of 302 U/L (7x ULN) during the washout period (29 days
after discontinuation of aficamten). His other liver tests were within the normal range, and his
AST returned to within the normal range after 2 weeks with no intervention. Two aficamtentreated subjects had elevated bilirubin >2x ULN (one >3x ULN); both episodes were transient
and occurred during the washout period about 4 weeks after discontinuation of aficamten.
Overall, the liver laboratory data did not raise findings of concern.
Figure 9 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury in
CY 6031. There were also no subjects who met Hy’s law criteria in the dose-ranging Study CY
6021 and the open-label extension Study CY 6022.
Figure 9. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Trial CY
6031

Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the
postbaseline period.
A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN criteria within 30
days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN criteria are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple’s corollary cases.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table “Subjects in Each Quadrant for Potential Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver
Injury Screening Plot …” and the listing “Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening…”.
Abbreviations: ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; ULN, upper limit of normal

7.6.1.10. Vital Signs
Analyses of vital signs data from CY 6031 found a greater mean change from baseline in
systolic/diastolic blood pressure (SBP/DBP) in the aficamten group compared to placebo (Figure
10 and Figure 11). The mean increases were sustained throughout the treatment period with ~2 to
4 mm Hg increase from baseline in the aficamten group compared to a 1 to 3 mm Hg decrease
from baseline in the placebo group.
Subjects with potentially clinically significant SBP and DBP are summarized in Table 37 and
Table 38, respectively. Elevated postbaseline SBP (≥160 mm Hg) was observed in
approximately 16% of subjects in the aficamten group and 8% of subjects in the placebo group.
Similarly, incidence of elevated postbaseline DBP (≥90 mm Hg) was higher in the aficamten
group compared to placebo (30% versus 20%). The shift tables by baseline SBP and DBP are
listed in Table 39 and Table 40 and do not raise additional concerns. All 11 subjects in the
aficamten group who reported hypertension AEs (Table 33) had at least one post-baseline
measurement of SBP ≥140 mm Hg.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Risk of Heart Failure Due to Systolic Dysfunction
Issue
The Applicant proposed to manage the iatrogenic risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction via
labeling and voluntary education materials without a REMS program at the time of the NDA
submission. Whether the proposed strategy is sufficient to ensure the safe use of aficamten was a
review issue.
Background
Systolic dysfunction is the identified risk for cardiac myosin inhibitors based on the mechanism
of action. The first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor was approved in 2022 with a REMS to
mitigate the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction. The objectives of the REMS were to ensure
periodic echocardiograms for dosing and monitoring and to screen for drug interactions that
would increase exposure, thus increasing the risk of systolic dysfunction. The Applicant stated
that aficamten has a PK and PD profile that minimizes the risk of iatrogenic systolic HF. The
available clinical data to date also demonstrate a low risk of HF and/or systolic dysfunction. In
light of these findings and the burden associated with a REMS, the Applicant proposed to
mitigate the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction without a REMS for aficamten.
Assessment
Clinical Pharmacologic Features of Aficamten
Due to its complex pharmacologic profile, the first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor requires
implementation of a complicated and structured dosing titration algorithm in conjunction with
the REMS. Aficamten has a terminal half-life of ~80 hours, with steady-state concentrations
achieved after 10-12 days of treatment. Aficamten undergoes elimination primarily through
CYP2C9-mediated metabolism (50%) with additional contributions from CYP3A (26%),
CYP2D6, and CYP2C19 pathways. The involvement of multiple CYP enzymes in aficamten
elimination reduces the likelihood of clinically significant concentration increases resulting from
potent inhibition of any single metabolic pathway. This suggests that large changes in aficamten
concentrations are less likely to emerge from strong inhibition of any individual pathway.
71
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Currently, there are no strong CYP2C9 inhibitors commercially available in the United States.
Aficamten exposure may be elevated with coadministration of strong inhibitors affecting
multiple CYPs (such as fluvoxamine, fluconazole, and voriconazole, see Section 14). Based on
these pharmacologic characteristics, aficamten demonstrates a moderate risk of increased
exposure, potentially supporting implementation of a less restrictive dosing strategy and
monitoring program.
Risk of Clinical Heart Failure/Systolic Dysfunction Features of Aficamten
There were no aficamten-treated oHCM subjects experiencing serious HF or severe systolic
dysfunction (LVEF <30%) in CY 6031 and in the open-label extension (OLE) CY 6022 study.
Three SAEs of HF/ventricular dysfunction have been reported in aficamten-treated subjects with
nHCM (see Narratives in Section 17.1). Table 41 summarizes the incidence and event rate of
SAEs of HF/systolic dysfunction among subjects with oHCM and nHCM in the aficamten
clinical programs at the time of the NDA submission for aficamten.
Table 41. Incidence and Event Rate of Serious Heart Failure in the Aficamten Clinical Program
Aficamten
(oHCM+nHCM)

Abbreviation: ER, event rate; N, number of subjects in the integrated summary of safety; n, number of subjects having an event;
LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; nHCM, non-obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; oHCM, obstructive hypertrophic
cardiomyopathy; SAE, serious adverse event

Safety Updates
The division requested that the Applicant report any case of SAE of HF and/or LVEF <30% in all
ongoing clinical trials throughout the NDA review period. No occurrences of LVEF <30% have
occurred in the aficamten clinical program through June 16, 2025. There were an additional six
SAEs of cardiac failure in an ongoing phase 3 trial CY 6033 in subjects with nHCM (treatment
assignments remain blinded) and two cases in subjects with oHCM (one on aficamten and the other
on metoprolol) in the second phase 3 trial comparing the effect of aficamten versus an active
control, metoprolol, in subjects with oHCM. Overall, no major concerns arose from these cases.
All cases were resolved with or without treatment interruptions.
Uncertainty
While the risk of serious HF or severe systolic dysfunction appears to be low in aficamtentreated oHCM patients, an iatrogenic risk of HF with aficamten still exists based on the
mechanism of action of the drug. The total exposure to date remains relatively low with
approximately 300 patients with oHCM. There is a concern that the risk would be higher in the
post-market setting where aficamten is used in a wider range of patients with higher
comorbidities. It should be noted that CY 6031 excluded patients with any history of LV systolic
dysfunction (LVEF <45%) or stress cardiomyopathy at any time during their clinical course. It is
not unreasonable to speculate that these excluded patients may be prescribed aficamten in the
real world. Further, this class of drug is relatively new, and prescribers are still learning about
72
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
this class and would benefit from mandated training. Careful monitoring in the real world is not
expected to be as rigorous as in the clinical trials, and labeling alone will not ensure that
prescribers appropriately titrate aficamten and obtain echocardiograms at appropriate time
points.
Conclusion
The review team determined that a REMS is necessary to ensure the benefits outweigh the
potential risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction. However, the review team concluded that the
PK profile of aficamten does not appear to warrant the inclusion of a drug interaction monitoring
component in a REMS. Dose modification related to certain strong inhibitors of multiple CYPs
can be addressed via labeling. The REMS Oversight Committee concurred with this decision and
recommended that prescriber education and documentation of echocardiogram monitoring must
be included as part of a REMS. Hence, a REMS with ETASU consisting of ETASU A
(prescriber certification), ETASU B (pharmacy certification), ETASU D (documentation of safeuse conditions), and ETASU E (patient monitoring) is required for aficamten. This decision on
REMS was conveyed to the Applicant at the Mid-Cycle Communication meeting on March 4,
2025, and the Applicant subsequently submitted their proposed REMS on March 28, 2025, in
response to the requirement (see the Division of Risk Management review), which was classified
as a major amendment to the application and extended the PDUFA goal date by 3 months.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic Impairment
The effect of moderate hepatic impairment (HI) on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten was
evaluated in a dedicated Phase 1 HI study (CY 6017). Aficamten exposures following a single
dose of aficamten 20 mg were similar in subjects with normal hepatic function and subjects with
moderate HI (i.e., Child-Pugh Class B) (i.e., 38% increase in mean Cmax and 5% decrease in
mean AUC∞ with moderate HI). Based on the outcome of this study, the Applicant concluded
that no adjustment of the aficamten dose is required for subjects with mild or moderate HI (i.e.,
Child-Pugh A or B). Details about this study can be found in Section 14.2 of this review. No
studies have been conducted in subjects with severe HI (i.e., Child-Pugh C).
While the Clinical Pharmacology and Clinical review team believes that there would not be
safety concerns with severe HI subjects as the aficamten dose will be titrated with safety
monitoring, the statement of “the effects of severe hepatic (Child-Pugh Class C) impairment is
unknown” has been included in the product label.
Renal Impairment
Based on the results of the mass balance study (CY 6013) that showed a minor role of renal
function in aficamten elimination (i.e., renal clearance <0.1% of total clearance), the Applicant
believes that clinically significant increases in aficamten exposure due to renal impairment (RI)

73
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
are unlikely and therefore, the effect of renal impairment on aficamten exposure was not
evaluated in a dedicated clinical study.
The Applicant evaluated the effect of mild or moderate RI on aficamten pharmacokinetics using
PPK analyses based on Phase 2 and 3 study data (link to Section 14.5). The aficamten Phase 2
and 3 studies (CY 6021 and CY 6031) included subjects with oHCM that had mild (i.e.,
creatinine clearance [CrCL; Cockcroft-Gault equation] of 60 to 89 mL/min; N=54) or moderate
(i.e., CrCL of 30 to 59 mL/min; N=13) RI. However, individuals with severe RI (i.e., estimated
glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] <30 mL/min/1.73 m2) were excluded from the Phase 2 and 3
studies. While evaluating the effect of renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics, the
Clinical Pharmacology review team noted a discrepancy between how the Applicant classified
renal impairment in the inclusion/exclusion criteria for the clinical studies and how their PPK
analysis was conducted. As a result, the following IR was sent to the Applicant on April 14,
2025:
We note that inclusion/exclusion criteria for renal impairment in your clinical studies
(e.g., Studies CY 6021 and CY 6031) were based on estimated glomerular filtration rate
(eGFR) using the modification of diet in renal disease (MDRD) equation while you
conducted population PK analysis using renal impairment classification based on
creatinine clearance (CLCr) derived from the Cockcroft-Gault (C-G) equation and used
that for labeling. Clarify whether using different equations to estimate renal function
would result in any difference in: (1) classification of renal function in the studied
population; and (2) the effect of renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics based
on your population PK analysis.

Subsequently, the Applicant submitted their response as follows on April 18, 2025 (SDN: 030):
“Cytokinetics has re-assessed the impact of renal function (eGFR estimated using MDRD
equation) on aficamten pharmacokinetics. When compared to the C-G-based assessment,
estimation of renal function using MDRD resulted in higher number of subjects being classified
as having mild or moderate renal impairment (i.e., N=54 with mild RI and N=13 with moderate
RI based on C-G equation). As expected for a drug that is eliminated primarily by metabolism,
aficamten pharmacokinetics was comparable among subjects with normal renal function and
those with mild or moderate renal impairment (Table 42). These results agree with those using
the C-G classification; thereby, supporting the use of aficamten in subjects with oHCM with
mild to moderate renal impairment.” The Applicant’s response was found to be reasonable from
a Clinical Pharmacology perspective.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)

The Pharmacometrics review team assessed the Applicant’s PPK analyses for the effect of mild
or moderate RI on aficamten pharmacokinetics. The PPK dataset is comprised of 54 subjects
with mild RI and 13 subjects with moderate RI. Based on the PPK analysis, creatinine clearance
was not identified as a significant covariate affecting aficamten pharmacokinetics. Similar
weight normalized clearance was observed in subjects with normal renal function and subjects
with mild and moderate RI (Figure 12). The steady-state PK comparison across renal function
suggested numerical increase in aficamten exposure with increasing degree of RI, which is likely
due to the decreased weight and more female subjects with increasing RI in the dataset. Overall,
the Applicant’s proposed dosing recommendations for subjects with mild and moderate RI is
reasonable.

largest/smallest value no further than 1.5 * IQR (inter-quartile range) from the box.

While the Clinical Pharmacology and Clinical review teams believe that there would not be
safety concerns with RI subjects as aficamten dose will be titrated with safety monitoring, the
statement of “the effects of severe renal (eGFR <30 mL/min based on MDRD equation)
impairment is unknown” has been included in the product label.
Age
Based on PPK-estimated aficamten exposures at steady-state following once daily (QD) dosing
of 15-mg aficamten (i.e., median last stable dose in Trial CY 6031) in subjects with oHCM
stratified by age group (i.e., <65 years, 65 to 75 years, and >75 years), a general numerical
increase in aficamten exposure was observed with increasing age (i.e., age: 18 to 83 years). The
Applicant believes that this result is likely due to the decreased body weight and increased
proportion of female subjects with increasing age group in the dataset. The Applicant concluded
that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on age. The Pharmacometrics review
team found this to be reasonable.
Body Weight
Body weight was identified as a significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics in the
Applicant’s PPK analysis. Population PK-estimated steady-state exposures (i.e., AUCtau, Cmax,
and Ctau) for aficamten in subjects with oHCM were compared across body weight quartiles.
Body weight was predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten
plasma AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau in a subject with oHCM at the fourth (i.e., highest) weight quartile
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
(i.e., 95 to 156 kg) were 34%, 35%, and 33% lower, respectively, compared to a subject with
oHCM at the first (i.e., lowest) weight quartile (i.e., 47 to 70 kg). The Applicant believes that
these differences are expected from simple allometry and the span of median weight between the
fourth and first weight quartiles. The Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment of aficamten
is warranted based on body weight. The Applicant believes that the proposed individualized
aficamten dose titration strategy based on achievement of target LVOT-G and maintenance of
normal LVEF is expected to mitigate any effect of body weight on aficamten pharmacokinetics.
The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
Sex
Sex was identified as a significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics in the Applicant’s
PPK analysis. Population PK-estimated steady-state exposures (i.e., AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau) for
aficamten in subjects with oHCM were compared by male versus female (i.e., 105 males and 78
females). Sex was predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten
plasma AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau in female subjects with oHCM were 31%, 34%, and 30% higher,
respectively, relative to male subjects with oHCM. The Applicant concludes that no dose
adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on sex considering that the general practice of
echocardiography-based aficamten dose titration is expected to mitigate any effect of sex on
aficamten pharmacokinetics. The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
Ethnicity
The impact of ethnicity on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten was evaluated via the Applicant’s
PPK analyses using the Phase 2 (Study CY 6021) and Phase 3 (Trial CY 6031) data. No impact
of ethnicity on aficamten pharmacokinetics was identified in the PPK analysis. Therefore, the
Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on ethnicity. The
Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
Overall Recommendation
The Clinical Pharmacology review team agrees that no dose adjustment for aficamten is
warranted for intrinsic factors such as HI, RI, age, body weight, sex, and ethnicity that are
described above.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Food
The effect of food on aficamten pharmacokinetics was assessed in a dedicated food effect study
(CY 6012) conducted with the to-be-marketed (TBM) tablet formulation to support that
aficamten may be administered without regard to food. No clinically significant differences in
aficamten exposure (i.e., AUC and Cmax) were observed following its administration with a highfat, high-calorie meal. Details of the study can be found in Section 14.2. Aficamten was
administered without regard to food in the pivotal Phase 3 trial, CY 6031.

77
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Drug-Drug Interactions
Effect of Other Drugs on Aficamten Pharmacokinetics
The DDI potential of aficamten was evaluated in vitro and in clinical studies.
In Vitro DDI Data
Based on the in vitro studies, aficamten is metabolized primarily through CYP2C9 with
contributions by CYP3A4, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. Aficamten is not a substrate of drug
transporters.
Clinical DDI Data
Based on the in vitro findings, several clinical DDI studies were conducted including those
evaluating the effects of strong CYP3A inhibition (i.e., itraconazole), CYP3A and CYP2C9
induction (i.e., carbamazepine) (Study CY 6014); strong inhibition of CYP2C19 and moderate
inhibition of CYP2C9 and CYP3A (i.e., fluconazole), strong inhibition of CYP2D6 (i.e.,
paroxetine), and strong inhibition of CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 (i.e., fluoxetine) (Study CY 60110) on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten (Table 43). Results from in vitro and clinical DDI
assessments indicate that aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9-mediated metabolism
(50%) with contributions from CYP3A (26%), CYP2C19, and CYP2D6.
Table 43. Observed Effect of Coadministered Drugs on the Pharmacokinetics of Aficamten in
Healthy Subjects, Studies CY 6014 and CY 601-10

Source: Table 43, Module 2.7.2

Physiologically Based PK Modeling
To further understand the effects of CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A inhibition and
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A induction on aficamten pharmacokinetics, a physiologically
78
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
based PK (PBPK) model was developed by the Applicant to supplement the clinical DDI studies
and provide a comprehensive evaluation of DDI effects on aficamten’s pharmacokinetics (see
Section 14.5.3).
In their PBPK report, CYT/11/B, the Applicant stated that their primary model fm values for
CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 can be considered verified, but there is still some uncertainty for the fm
values for CYP2C19 and CYP2D6. The over-prediction of aficamten pharmacokinetics in
CYP2D6 poor metabolizers (PMs) and the underprediction of the fluconazole DDI may both
indicate a potential greater role of CYP2C19 (i.e., estimated maximum fm =24%) and lesser role
for CYP2D6 (i.e., fm =0%). A sensitivity analysis performed using an exploratory model with
CYP2D6 mediated metabolism removed and replaced by CYP2C19 mediated metabolism was
also presented in their PBPK report (CYT/11/B). As expected, this model more accurately
describes the pharmacokinetics in CYP2D6 PMs (i.e., no change from non-PMs) and the
fluconazole DDI (i.e., predicted within 1.1-fold of the observed data) but no longer captures the
effect of paroxetine DDI. As such, the Applicant relied on a model (i.e., primary model) that
includes a significant contribution by CYP2D6 to align with in vitro results and accurately
capture the results of the dedicated DDI study with paroxetine for prospective DDI prediction.
While evaluating the Applicant’s PBPK modeling, the Clinical Pharmacology PBPK reviewer
further modified the Applicant’s exploratory model by assigning fmCYP2D6 to 3% and fmCYP2C19
to 21% and making further necessary adjustments (see Section 14.5.3). As a result, the
reviewer’s model successfully predicted all changes in aficamten AUCs observed in clinical
studies including paroxetine. The mean AUC changes predicted by the aforementioned three
different PBPK models are summarized in Table 44. In addition, different phenotypes of
CYP2C9 (normal metabolizers [NMs] and PMs) were considered to account for the worst-case
scenario for aficamten exposure increase. For example, we compared aficamten AUC in
CYP2C9 PMs in the presence of an inhibitor to aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 NMs in the absence
of the inhibitor. The combined inhibition effect (i.e., aficamten AUC increase in PMs in the
presence of an inhibitor) will be a product of multiplying the baseline difference between PMs
and NMs with the exposure increase resulting from the coadministration with an inhibitor. For
CYP2C9, the effect of sulfaphenazole (i.e., strong CYP2C9 inhibitor) on aficamten AUC in
CYP2C9 NMs was considered as the baseline difference in AUC between CYP2C9 NMs and
PMs (i.e., 2.13-fold). For example, aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 PMs following coadministration
with fluvoxamine 150-mg BID for 15 days is predicted to be approximately 4.9-fold higher
compared to CYP2C9 NMs not taking fluvoxamine when using the reviewer’s model in Table 44
(i.e., 2.13 [NM versus PM baseline difference]×2.28 [aficamten AUC increase with
fluvoxamine]).

Reference ID: 5715605

Dosing Recommendations Based on DDI Potential Affecting Aficamten Pharmacokinetics
This section focuses on dosing recommendations for aficamten when dose adjustment is needed
based on DDI potential with other drugs given concomitantly. While drug interaction potential
may exist for other drugs when given with aficamten, aficamten dose adjustments are not
warranted unless discussed in this section.
Table 45 summarizes predictions of aficamten mean AUC fold increase when coadministered
with various CYP enzyme inhibitors together with the Applicant’s proposed dose
recommendations and the Clinical Pharmacology review team’s dose recommendations.
Table 45. Aficamten Mean AUC Increase Predictions Based on Different PBPK Models and the
Clinical Pharmacology Review Team’s Dose Recommendations

Source: FDA reviewer’s analysis
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time-curve; BID, twice daily; CYP, cytochrome P450; LVEF, left ventricular
ejection fraction; LVOT-G, left ventricular outflow tract gradient; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetic; QD, once daily

80
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
The Applicant proposed to contraindicate aficamten administration with more than 3 days of
fluconazole administration, regardless of CYP2C9 phenotype. This is based on the observation
that fluconazole 400-mg QD increased aficamten exposure by 3.8-fold in CYP2C9 and
CYP2C19 extensive, rapid, or ultra-rapid metabolizers (Study CY 601-10). As shown in Table
45 above, the combined effects of multiple CYP enzyme inhibition and CYP2C9 PM status for
voriconazole and fluconazole are predicted to result in a 7.5-fold and 7.6-fold higher AUC,
respectively. Therefore, when initiating voriconazole or fluconazole in subjects already taking
aficamten, the aficamten dose should be reduced to 5 mg (i.e., 3 to 4-fold dose decrease from
15 mg or 20-mg dose) considering approximately 3.5-fold AUC increase expected by initiating
these inhibitors. Aficamten is expected to have more than a 3-fold therapeutic window based on
the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) outcome. The proposed 3 to 4-fold aficamten dose reduction is
expected to appropriately mitigate any resulting additional aficamten exposure increases due to
the initiation of these inhibitors. However, those on 5 mg or 10-mg aficamten should avoid
concomitant use of voriconazole or fluconazole as reduction of the aficamten dose may not
appropriately mitigate the risk resulting from aficamten exposure increase.
At the same time, the Applicant proposes to allow ≤3 days of concomitant fluconazole to enable
the common usage of fluconazole in the outpatient setting for the treatment of uncomplicated
vulvovaginal candidiasis. In this setting, fluconazole is administered as a single 150-mg dose
(Day 1). If the infection does not resolve within 48 hours, a second 150-mg dose (Day 3) can be
given. The effect of 2×150-mg fluconazole dose, separated by 48 hours, on stable aficamten
therapy (i.e., steady state 15-mg QD) was evaluated via PBPK modeling; and it was predicted
that this regimen will only elicit a minimal effect on aficamten exposure (i.e., maximum increase
of 10% AUC over the dosing interval). The Clinical Pharmacology review team found the
Applicant’s proposal to be reasonable.
The aficamten dose should be reduced 2 to 3-fold when initiating fluvoxamine or sulfaphenzole.
In the same manner as when initiating inhibitors such as voriconazole or fluconazole, a 2 to 3fold aficamten dose reduction is expected to appropriately mitigate any resulting additional
aficamten exposure increase due to the initiation of these inhibitors in subjects already taking
aficamten considering that less than a 2.3-fold mean AUC increase is expected by initiating these
inhibitors.
When initiating aficamten in subjects already taking the above-mentioned inhibitors, aficamten
should be initiated at 5 mg with LVEF and LVOT-G monitoring and the dose should be
increased further if needed based on treatment response. A starting dose of 5 mg is expected to
be safe for aficamten despite an approximately 2-fold decrease expected in aficamten clearance
due to inhibitor presence as there would not be any additional exposure increase due to the
initiation of aficamten therapy as subjects are already on stable therapy with the abovementioned inhibitors. Any subsequent aficamten dose up-titration while on inhibitor therapy can
be safely guided by echocardiographic assessment to maintain LVEF >50%.
To assess the potential effect of CYP inducers on aficamten pharmacokinetics, the Applicant
conducted a PBPK analysis showing that 600-mg QD dosing of rifampin (i.e., strong CYP3A
inducer, strong CYP2C19 inducer, and moderate CYP2C9 inducer) was predicted to reduce the
mean aficamten AUC by 79%. Given the extent of predicted drug interaction with rifampin, the
Applicant is proposing to contraindicate the concomitant use of aficamten with rifampin as the
reduced aficamten exposure may substantially reduce efficacy.
81
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
A clinical DDI study (CY 6014) was conducted to evaluate the effect of steady state
carbamazepine (i.e., moderate to strong CYP3A inducer and weak CYP2C9 inducer) exposure
on aficamten single dose (i.e., 20 mg) pharmacokinetics. This study showed a 51% decrease in
aficamten mean AUC. The Applicant is proposing to reduce the dose of aficamten (i.e., 20 mg →
10 mg, 15 mg → 5 mg, 10 mg → 5 mg, or maintain the dose of 5 mg if currently on 5 mg and
monitor LVEF and LVOT-G) when discontinuing a moderate to strong inducer of CYP2C9 or
CYP3A.
The Applicant’s proposal for both inducer drugs mentioned above is reasonable from a Clinical
Pharmacology perspective.
After careful consideration, the Clinical Pharmacology review team has concluded that the
listing of CYP2C9 inducers in Section 7 (i.e., Drug Interactions) of the product label is not
necessary as there are no strong CYP2C9 inducers currently on the United States market (i.e.,
hypothetical) and other known moderate CYP2C9 inducers would have overlap with moderate to
strong CYP3A inducers.
It should be noted that no significant exposure-safety relationship was identified between
aficamten exposures and assessed safety endpoints such as major adverse cardiovascular events,
cardiovascular adverse events, or hypertension at the evaluated dose range of 5 to 20-mg QD in
the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) and the aficamten therapeutic window is expected to be >3-fold.
Effect of Aficamten on Other Drugs’ Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant concludes that in vitro studies suggest aficamten does not substantially inhibit any
of the CYP isoforms tested (i.e., CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
and CYP3A4; IC50 values >100μM), and it is predicted not to be an in vivo inducer in humans.
The in vitro assessment of the inhibition potential for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer
resistance protein (BCRP), organic cation transporter 2 (OCT2), organic cation transporter 3
(OCT3), organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1), organic anion transporter 3 (OAT3), organic anion
transporting polypeptide 1B1 (OATP1B1), organic anion transporting polypeptide 1B3
(OATP1B3), multidrug and toxin extrusion protein 1 (MATE1), and multidrug and toxin
extrusion protein 2-K (MATE2-K) suggested that aficamten may inhibit P-gp in vivo, leading to
evaluation in a dedicated clinical DDI study (Study CY 6014). Concomitant administration of
aficamten increased total dabigatran (i.e., a sensitive P-gp probe) AUC∞ and Cmax by 26% and
27%, respectively (Section 14.2). Based on these results, the review team agrees with the
Applicant’s conclusion that aficamten may be coadministered with P-gp substrates.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Aficamten was granted orphan designation for the treatment of HCM on January 5, 2021. Based
on the orphan designation for aficamten and in accordance with Section 505B(k)(1) of the Food,
Drug, and Cosmetic (FD&C) Act, a formal pediatric study plan for the population of birth to less
than 18 years of age is not required. This exemption from the Pediatric Research Equity Act
requirements was confirmed at the pre-NDA meeting held on February 26, 2024.

MYQORZO (aficamten)

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Refer to the Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Heath review dated June 4, 2025, and the
summary of key nonclinical data (Table 46) supporting the labeling sections on fertility,
pregnancy and lactation.
A rat embryofetal development (EFD) study demonstrated an increase in external malformations
(kinked tail) at aficamten exposures higher than clinical exposures at the MRHD. This finding is
of uncertain clinical relevance.
In a rabbit EFD study, a numerical imbalance in cardiac findings was observed between the middose and vehicle control groups (two litters versus one, respectively). The findings consisted of
increased cardiac malformations (persistent truncus arteriosus, misshapen heart, and absent
ventricular septum) that were slightly above the historical control incidence. The findings were
not observed with a clear dose-response (even when accounting for the loss of some litters at the
highest dose). Furthermore, all three malformations also occurred in control litters, which
indicates the findings are not rare (i.e., they occurred at 5 to 6% in the historical control database,
a level exceeding a threshold of >1 to 2%). Furthermore, imbalances in cardiac malformations
were not observed in fetuses in the rat EFD study. Taken together, while a direct relationship
between aficamten and cardiac malformations cannot be entirely excluded (due primarily to
aficamten’s mechanism of action), the available data are inadequate to establish a causal
relationship. Given the signal cannot be distinguished from a chance finding, we recommend that
residual concern be mitigated through disclosure of the findings in product labeling.
Animal fertility studies do not demonstrate any adverse effect at clinical exposures, therefore,
aficamten is not predicted to impair human fertility.
Given the limited human data on aficamten use in pregnancy and the absence of data regarding
aficamten presence in human breast milk, the Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Heath
recommends issuing two post marketing requirements to conduct studies that collect pregnancy
outcome data in humans and determine aficamten concentrations in human breast milk (see
Section 24).

Administration of aficamten during organogenesis in rats was associated
with increased early and late fetal resorptions, decreased mean fetal body

13.1 Impairment of Fertility

Administration of aficamten during organogenesis in rabbits was
associated with a numerical increase in postimplantation loss (late
resorptions) and fetuses with various cardiac malformations at the middose (10 mg/kg) only. The high dose (20/15 mg/kg/day) caused maternal
toxicity and mortalities at a clinically relevant exposure level.
Administration of aficamten to pregnant rats during embryofetal
organogenesis through offspring weaning in a pre- and postnatal
development study caused reduced offspring viability and reduced pup
birth weight at the highest dose (6 mg/kg/day) in the presence of maternal
toxicities (decreased maternal body weight gain and increased maternal
heart weight), at about 4-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD. An
increased number of offspring with bent tails was also observed at the
same dose. No adverse maternal toxicity or developmental effects
occurred at the mid-dose at clinical exposure level. Aficamten did not
affect neurobehavioral parameters, sexual maturation, or reproductive
function of the offspring at any dose.
In a rat PPND study, aficamten was detected in nursing offspring when
administered to lactating mothers from gestation through postnatal day 4
at ≥0.5 mg/kg/day.
Aficamten exposure did not impact fertility, mating frequency or index,
estrus, sperm quality, or histopathology of reproductive organs in both
male and female in rats, at up to 6 mg/kg/day in females and 3 mg/kg/day
in males.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
The IND opening study, CY 6011, entitled “A Phase 1, Double-blind, Randomized, Placebocontrolled, Multi-part, Single and Multiple-Ascending Dose Study of CK-3773274 in Healthy
Adult Subjects” was submitted on October 3, 2018, and a study may proceed letter was issued on
November 2, 2018.
Aficamten received Orphan Drug Designation on January 5, 2021, for the treatment of
symptomatic hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Aficamten also was granted breakthrough therapy
designation on December 6, 2021.
Trial CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM), entitled "A Phase 3, Multi-Center, Randomized, Doubleblind, Placebo-Controlled Trial to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of CK-3773274 in Adults
with Symptomatic oHCM" was conducted globally, including in the United States. The
enrollment of Trial CY 6031 was completed on May 26, 2023. While there were no interim data
locks, the protocol specified that the Applicant would monitor the aggregate pooled missing data
and overall pooled standard deviation for the change in baseline in pVO2 at Week 24 (the
primary endpoint). The Applicant did not lock the database to perform this monitoring, nor did
they adjust the sample size. The results of CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM) and the additional safety
data from the open-label extension Study CY 6022 (FOREST-HCM), to support the NDA
submission for aficamten in patients with symptomatic oHCM were discussed with the division
at the topline meeting on February 8, 2024 (minutes dated February 29, 2024), and the pre-NDA
meeting held on February 26, 2024 (minutes dated March 26, 2024). The review team concluded
the information outlined in the pre-NDA meeting package appeared sufficient to conduct a filing
review of the NDA and accepted a rolling review with the clinical and nonclinical modules
ahead of the full NDA submission.
On September 26, 2024, Cytokinetics Inc. submitted the final portion of the Rolling submission
of their NDA for aficamten for the following proposed indication: “treatment of adults with
symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (oHCM)”.
Please see the key interactions with the Agency over the course of development, provided in

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
Aficamten is a small molecule inhibitor of cardiac myosin (Myh7) designed to decrease
cardiomyocyte contractility and relax the cardiac muscle for the treatment of oHCM. Results
from in vitro and in vivo primary pharmacology studies support the proposed mechanism of
action, the results of which are summarized in Table 48. The selectivity and dose-response
profiles of aficamten and its metabolites were investigated using purified myofibrils, cultured
cardiac cells, in engineered cardiac tissue, and in both healthy animals and animal models of
oHCM.

13.1.1. Safety Pharmacology
A complete battery of safety pharmacology studies was conducted to characterize any effects of
aficamten and its metabolites on the human ether-à-go-go related gene (hERG) channel,
cardiovascular, respiratory, and central nervous system. Effects observed on electrocardiogram
readings after repeated aficamten dosing are typical of cardiac compensation due to the intended
pharmacologic action of aficamten. Aficamten and its metabolites did not have inhibitory effects
on the hERG channel at clinically relevant concentrations. The safety pharmacology studies are
summarized in Table 50 below.

13.1.2. Pharmacokinetics
The pharmacokinetics and absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) of
aficamten were characterized in in vitro assays and in vivo studies using pharmacologically
relevant species that included the dog, rat, rabbit, mouse, and monkey. Toxicokinetics were
assessed in toxicology studies in rats, dogs, rabbits, and mice. Overall, these data confirm the
clinical relevance of these nonclinical models for human risk assessment for aficamten.
13.1.2.1. Absorption and Distribution
Absorption of aficamten after oral dosing was comparable between rats, dogs, and primates.
Absolute oral bioavailability was ~60% for rats, ~40% for dogs and non-human primates, and
was not determined for humans. Time to maximum concentration (Tmax) in humans after dosing
with the clinical formulation was 1.5 to 2 hours, which was slightly longer than Tmax in rats and
dogs dosed by oral gavage (~0.5 to 1 hour). Steady-state volume of distribution between species
varied widely, from 0.5 L/kg in rats to 11 L/kg in dogs and 8 L/kg in non-human primates. No
significant differences in aficamten exposure between males and females for any species were
observed.
Plasma protein binding for aficamten and its major metabolites (M1a and M1b) were determined
for several species in vitro. Aficamten plasma protein binding was 98%, 90%, and 75% in rats,
rabbits, and dogs, respectively. M1a and M1b protein binding was ~86% and ~89% in rats, and
~45% and 43% in dogs, respectively. Human plasma protein binding in vitro for aficamten, M1a,
and M1b were ~90%, ~83%, and ~93% respectively. To account for the differences in plasma
protein binding between species, exposure margins for aficamten were calculated based on
unbound (free) Cmax or AUC in addition to total Cmax and total AUC.
After a single oral dose in fasted rats, aficamten was detected in the heart, plasma, liver, and
gastrocnemius muscle, with very low levels detected in the cerebrospinal fluid and brain. The
tissue with highest exposure to aficamten was the heart, the site of aficamten’s pharmacological
action, with a tissue-to-plasma ratio of ~1.4, followed by the gastrocnemius with a ratio of 0.7.
Notably, the heart was the primary organ of toxicity in animal toxicology studies.
Placental and milk transfer of aficamten was not directly assessed in embryofetal development
study or pre- and postnatal development studies.
13.1.2.2. Metabolism and Excretion
After oral dosing, aficamten is metabolized more quickly in animals as compared to humans.
Aficamten’s half-life was 25 hours in rats, 79 hours in dogs, and 144 hours in human hepatocytes
90
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
in vitro. Half-life measurements across species in vivo are ~80 hours, ~28 hours, and ~3 hours, in
humans, dogs, and rats, respectively. Drug accumulation was evident in repeat-dose studies in
dogs by exposure ratios of ~2 when comparing AUC and ~1.5 when comparing Cmax on the final
day of dosing to the first day. Daily dosing in the clinic resulted in drug accumulation in humans
as well, with an exposure ratio of ~5 based on AUC and ~2 based on Cmax, consistent with longer
half-life in humans.
In all species, the major circulating metabolites of aficamten were M1a and M1b (also known as
CK-3834282 and CK-3834283, respectively), diastereomers of (ω-1)-hydroxylation of the ethyl
group on the ethyl-1,2,4-oxadiazol-3-yl moiety of aficamten, formed by CYP enzymes. In
humans, metabolite to parent ratios for M1a and M1b were 74% and 129%, respectively. These
two metabolites are also major metabolites of aficamten identified in the rat in vivo, but at lower
levels. In rats, the metabolite to parent ratios for M1a and M1b were ~9% and 29%, respectively.
No unique human metabolites were identified in vitro using liver microsomes or in vivo, making
the rat and dog metabolically relevant species for toxicology studies.
M1a and M1b were inactive in all assays tested. In vitro PD assays and 14-day repeat dose
toxicology studies in rats with the major metabolites of aficamten demonstrated their relative
pharmacologic inactivity. In an in vitro study, M1b inhibited bovine cardiac myofibril ATPase
activity by 15% at 18µM, but no IC50 could be determined for M1a or M1b up to maximum
concentrations of 39µM, demonstrating lower potency by at least 30-fold, compared with
aficamten (IC50=1.26µM), which is anticipated to be pharmacologically inactive at therapeutic
exposures of aficamten. M1a or M1b singly administered to rats for 14 days at 50 mg/kg/day had
no toxicologically significant effects.
M1a and M1b were measured in aficamten repeat dose studies in rats and dogs. Exposures to
each metabolite relative to clinical levels are summarized in Table 51.

92
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Across the nonclinical studies included in Table 51, both M1a and M1b metabolites generally
demonstrated exposures exceeding 0.5-fold relative to the clinical exposure at the 20mg dose, a
threshold typically warranted to achieve adequate nonclinical assessment for disproportionate
metabolites. The exception was M1a exposure in male animals in carcinogenicity studies, where
exposures were limited to 0.2-fold in the 6-month transgenic mouse study and 0.3-fold in the 2year rat study. This lower exposure was attributed to two factors: cardiac toxicities associated
with aficamten that limited the high dose selection, and the inherently lower M1a levels present
in rodent species relative to humans, after aficamten administration. Given the higher maximum
tolerated dose of aficamten in females, M1a exposure in females reached 0.5-fold relative to
clinical exposure without tumor findings in the 2-year rat carcinogenicity study. Mechanistically
the M1a metabolite is formed through first-pass metabolism by P450-mediated hydroxylation
and is structurally very similar to aficamten, which was negative for tumor signals in 6-month
and 2-year rodent carcinogenicity studies.
M1a is pharmacologically inactive, and nonclinical rodent studies achieving exposures above
0.5-fold (including the 13-week rat study) showed no proliferative or preneoplastic findings of
concern. The 2-year rat study, which reached 0.5-fold exposure, similarly demonstrated no
concerning signals. Furthermore, M1a was determined to be non-genotoxic based on a
comprehensive battery of assays and weight-of-evidence evaluation. Collectively, these data
indicate minimal carcinogenic risk for this metabolite, therefore, we conclude that a dedicated
carcinogenicity study is not warranted.
13.1.2.3. Drug-Drug Interactions
Aficamten was not a substrate for OATP1B1/B3, MDR1/BCRP, or P-gp in vitro. In a transporter
panel inhibition screen, aficamten and its major metabolites had no significant effects on the
activity of OAT1, OCT2, OCT3, or OATP1B3. The IC50 for aficamten against OAT3,
OATP1B1, MATE1, and MATE2-K were 12, 71, 36, and 83µM, respectively. In vitro CYP
phenotyping, induction, and inhibition studies using recombinant CYP proteins, human liver
microsomes, human hepatocytes, or a human microliver model were performed. Results from the
studies collectively suggest that CYPs 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4 contribute to metabolic
clearance of aficamten in humans. Aficamten did not significantly inhibit any CYP enzyme in in
vitro assays. Based on thresholds described in the FDA guidance for industry Clinical Drug
Interaction Studies—Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions
(January 2020),5 the in vivo risk for inhibition by aficamten is low.

13.1.3. General Toxicology
Repeat oral dose toxicology studies up to 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in dogs were conducted
to support the chronic oral administration of aficamten intended for clinical use. Both species are
considered pharmacologically relevant given the high conservation of the cardiac myosin motor
between species and based on in vitro and single-dose in vivo pharmacology studies. Exposure

5

Guidances are updated periodically. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page
at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fdaguidance-documents.

93
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
multiples were estimated based on systemic exposure to total and unbound (free) aficamten
(AUC and Cmax) at the maximum recommended human clinical dose of 20 mg.
Repeat dosing at the clinical exposure based on free AUC (5-times greater than clinical exposure
based on total AUC) for up to 6-months in rats was well tolerated. In shorter 28-day and 13-week
rat studies, dosing at 2-fold greater than clinical free AUC level (8-times greater than clinical
total AUC) was tolerated, while dilated cardiomyopathy and premature deaths were associated
with dosing at 4-fold greater than clinical free AUC (14-times greater than clinical total AUC).
Similarly, in the 39-week dog study dosing to the clinical free AUC level caused no toxicities
(0.25-times the clinical total AUC). However, a dose level 2.5-fold greater than clinical free
AUC in dogs (0.6-times the clinical total AUC) had reversible increased heart weight. In a
shorter 13-week dog study, dosing at 4-fold clinical free AUC (at clinical total AUC) was
associated with cardiomyocyte necrosis and premature death. Taken together, the repeat-dose
toxicology studies demonstrate dose and duration-dependent cardiac toxicities in healthy animals
given daily aficamten.
FDA Assessment
The heart was identified as the primary organ of toxicity in both species with repeated aficamten
administrations. Cardiac toxicities culminating in heart failure resulting from exaggerated
pharmacology of the drug were observed in both rats and dogs in a dose- and duration-dependent
fashion. Drug exposures causing cardiac toxicity for both species were >2-fold and >3-fold the
maximum clinical exposures based on free AUC and free Cmax, respectively. Cardiac toxicities in
dogs were evident at clinical exposure based on total AUC and 2-times clinical exposure based
on total Cmax. In rats, cardiac toxicity occurred at 14-times clinical exposure based on total AUC
and 32-times clinical exposure based on total Cmax.
No adverse effects for either species were observed with chronic dosing at clinical exposure
based on free AUC and up to 3-fold based on free Cmax. No adverse findings occurred in dogs at
exposures less than clinical total AUC and total Cmax. For rats, no adverse findings occurred
when dosing up to 5-times total AUC and 9-times total Cmax. Hypercontractility is one of the
pathological hallmarks of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, and the indicated patient population
would likely tolerate a reduction in cardiac contractility to a greater extent than animals or
humans with 'normal' cardiac contractility. As such, while the nonclinical toxicity studies clearly
demonstrate that aficamten is capable of inhibiting myosin/actin interactions with a narrow
safety margin and, in excess, can precipitate heart failure, a broader therapeutic window may be
present in the intended patient population.

13.1.4. Genetic Toxicology
A GLP-compliant Ames assay and a GLP combined in vivo mammalian erythrocyte
micronucleus test and alkaline comet assay in rats were conducted in accordance with the
guidelines outlined in the International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) guidance for industry,
S1(R1) S1B(R1) Addendum to S1B Testing for Carcinogenicity of Pharmaceuticals (November
2022b). Aficamten was negative for genotoxicity and clastogenicity in both systems. GLPcompliant Ames assays and in vitro micronucleus assays were conducted for the two major
metabolites of aficamten, with a follow-up combined in vivo micronucleus assay and comet
assay conducted for metabolite M1a. Collectively, the metabolites were also negative for
genotoxicity and clastogenicity. See Table 57 for details.

Aficamten had no effects on fertility, mating, or reproductive assessments up to 6 mg/kg/day in

13.1.4.1.1. Carcinogenicity
GLP-compliant carcinogenicity studies of 6 months in transgenic rasH2 mice (Study Nonclin0306) and 2 years in Sprague Dawley rats (Study Nonclin-0248) were conducted after Executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (ECAC) concurrence regarding dose selection.
Aficamten was associated with an apparent dose-dependent trend for bronchioloalveolar
adenomas in male transgenic mice in the 6-month study with dosing up to 2 mg/kg/day
corresponding to a clinical exposure margin of 2-fold based on free AUC (0.6-fold based on total
AUC). The trend and pairwise comparisons between vehicle treated and aficamten treated
animals were not statistically significant based on ECAC statistical criteria. In a 2-year rat
carcinogenicity study, low survivability at the high dose for both sexes necessitated early
stoppage of dosing and study termination after ECAC concurrence. No aficamten-related
hyperplastic or neoplastic microscopic findings were noted for any organ in the 2-year rat study,
up to 2 mg/kg/day in males and 3 mg/kg/day in females corresponding to clinical exposure
margins of 2.5-fold and 4-fold based on free AUC, respectively (8-fold and 15-fold based on
male and female total AUC, respectively).
13.1.4.2. Reproductive Toxicology
GLP-compliant reproductive toxicology studies including a fertility and early embryonic
development study in rats, embryofetal development studies in rats and rabbits, and a pre- and
post-natal development study in rats were conducted with aficamten. Details of these studies are
summarized below in Table 59.

MYQORZO (aficamten)
Study Number/Title: Nonclin-0161/CK-3773274: An Oral Embryofetal Development Study
in Rats (GLP)
Key Study Findings


Statistically significant increased maternal mean absolute heart weight was observed at 6 and
9 mg/kg/day (up to 13% increase over controls).



Maternal toxicity characterized by decreased body weight gain, reduced food consumption,
and decreased terminal body weight at 9 mg/kg/day, which reached statistical significance at
sporadic intervals throughout the study, were adverse (up to 30% reduced food consumption,
64% reduced body weight gain).



The mean total fetuses per litter was reduced at 9 mg/kg/day, with mean postimplantation
loss of ~59% and a mean early resorption rate of ~7%. Three incidences of total litter loss,
and one incidence of abnormal placenta (pale foci) occurred at the same dose. See Table 63.



Surviving fetuses from the 9 mg/kg/day group had statistically significant reduction in weight
compared to controls, with increased incidence of kinked and short tails. See Table 63.



The NOAEL from this study was 6 mg/kg/day for both maternal and fetal toxicity with
systemic exposures of 93,900 and 1,502 ng∙h/mL for total and free AUC, respectively, and
10,400 and 166 ng/mL for total and free Cmax, respectively. Clinical safety margins based on
total and free AUC are 12X and 3.5X, respectively. Clinical safety margins based on total
and free Cmax are 32X and 9X, respectively.

Maternal toxicity
Pregnant females in the mid- and high-dose group had dosedependent lower body weight gain compared to vehicle

the high dose had increased incidence of kinked or short
tails. There was an increase in incidence of incomplete
zygomatic arch ossifications in the mid dose group
compared to vehicle controls, but it was within historical
control range.

Study Number/Title: Nonclin-0199/CK-3773274: An Oral Embryofetal Development Study
in Rabbits
Key Study Findings


There were dose-dependent reductions in maternal body weight gain, which were not
associated with decreased food consumption between Days 7 to 10 postcoital for all groups
(7, 17, 27% reduction compared to controls), which persisted for the high dose group through
Day 20 postcoital (overall body weight reduction of 11% compared to controls).



Statistically significant increased late resorptions occurred for the mid dose only, with mean
postimplantation loss occurring at a rate (10.5%) toward the high end of the historical control
values (1.3 to 10.7%). See Table 67.



Fetal cardiovascular malformations including dilated aorta, absent ventricular septation,
misshapen heart, retroesophageal subclavian artery, and truncus arteriosus occurred in the
mid-dose group only (10 mg/kg/day) at incidences exceeding the historical control range.
Truncus arteriosus was found in litters from the high dose group (20/15 mg/kg/day) at a rate
(0.6%) toward the high end of the historical control range (0.61%). See Table 67.



The maternal and embryofetal NOAEL doses were 10 mg/kg/day and 15 mg/kg/day,
respectively, with exposure margins below clinical exposure at the maximum recommended
human dose; doses higher that 15 mg/kg/day were not tolerated in rabbits.



The embryofetal NOAEL is based on the following:
– A slight numerical increase in the incidence (litters) of cardiovascular malformations
above concurrent and historical controls was observed in the mid-dose group.
– The imbalance was not dose-related and findings were absent or had similar incidence
between control and high-dose groups.
– We considered whether the four litters lost (on Days 7 to 9 post-coital) at the
20/15 mg/kg high dose may have the potential to confound the analysis.


Importantly, the missing litters were clearly due to maternal toxicity and death and
were not related to embryofetal toxicity (per se). Furthermore, postimplantation loss
and total number of fetuses (per litter) in surviving females was similar to controls.
These data indicate it was unlikely that maternal deaths confounded our analysis.

In conclusion, the incidence of malformations was deemed to be similar across dose groups,
similar to the concurrent and/or historical control levels, lacking in any apparent dose-response,
and unlikely to have been caused by lost litters (due to maternal deaths, not abortion/resorption),
106
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
and thus could not be attributed to aficamten, despite a signal reported for the first-in-class
cardiac myosin inhibitor, mavacamten.

Major Findings
Endpoints with notable
observations
Maternal toxicity
Three animals in the high dose group (20 mg/kg/day) died by postcoital Day
8, with an additional one on postcoital Day 9 after reducing the dose to

breathing, and muzzle discharge. In the low dose group, one animal was

activity, thin appearance, and abdominal distension. Reductions in body
weight gain were reported for all dose groups early in the study, without
accompanying increased cardiac weights.

mid-dose at a rate slightly above the historical control range, with no
apparent dose-dependent trend. The relation to aficamten is questionable
given the minor increase above controls, overall low total incidence, and
lack of dose-dependence.

Study Number/Title: Nonclin-0360/An Oral (Gavage) Study of the Effects of CK-3773274
on Pre- and Postnatal Development in Rats
Key Study Findings


At the high dose, 6 mg/kg/day, mean maternal heart weight was increased relative to controls
by 22%. Body weight gain during the gestation period was also reduced compared to controls
at the high dose, with accompanying decreased pup viability after birth and clinical signs
including absent milk band, thin appearance, and bent tail. The maternal and offspring
NOAELs were at the mid dose, 1.5 mg/kg/day. See Table 71.



Aficamten was detected in plasma from nursing postnatal Day 4 pups at all doses, suggesting
transfer through milk as the half-life of aficamten in rats is ~8 hours.

13.1.5. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
Major metabolites (M1a and M1b) of aficamten were characterized in a non-GLP 14-day repeat
dose study in rats, GLP Ames assays, and GLP in vivo and in vitro genetic toxicology studies. In
addition, aficamten was evaluated in a phototoxicity assay in vitro, and skin sensitization and
corrosion assays. In general, the major aficamten metabolites were well tolerated, not
pharmacodynamically active in vivo, and not genotoxic or clastogenic at clinically relevant
levels. Aficamten was not irritating or corrosive to the eye or skin in any study conducted. The
results of these studies are summarized below:
13.1.5.1. Metabolites
Study Number/Title: CK-3834282 and CK-3834283/A 14-Day Oral Dose Tolerability Study
in Sprague Dawley Rat (Nonclin-0221) (Non-GLP)


Major aficamten metabolites were administered separately by oral gavage to rats for 14
consecutive days at 50 mg/kg/day.



No remarkable clinical observations or effects on heart weight were found for either
metabolite up to Cmax levels of 13,933 ng/mL for males and 28,032 ng/mL for females for
M1a, and Cmax levels of 45,197 ng/mL for males and 62,663 ng/mL for females for M1b.
AUC0-24h values for males and females were 35,679 and 59,799 ng∙h/mL for M1a, and
215,085 and 287,778 ng∙h/mL for M1b, respectively.



The major aficamten metabolites were not pharmacologically active in vivo up to clinical
exposure multiples >100-times based on steady state Cmax, and >10-times based on steady
state AUC from clinical Study CY 6021 at an aficamten dose of 20 mg.

Study Number/Title: CK-3834282/Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay (Study Nonclin-0231)
and CK-3834283: Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay (Study Nonclin-0234)


Bacterial tester strains were grown with metabolite M1a or M1b up to 5000 µg/plate in the
presence or absence of rat liver S9.



Test-article precipitation was observed for both M1a and M1b at 3,333 µg/plate and
5000 µg/plate, respectively.



M1a did not induce an increase in revertant bacterial colonies up to 3,333 µg/plate. Toxicity
for M1a was noted at ≥3,333 µg/plate in the absence of S9.

112
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)


Growth of S. typhimurium strain TA98 with M1b at 100 µg/mL induced a 1.8X increase in
revertant colonies compared to controls in the absence of S9, but there was no dosedependent increase in revertant colonies above that dose.



The Ames assay was negative for both M1a and M1b under the conditions of these studies.

Study Number/Title: CK-3834282/In Vitro Mammalian Cell Micronucleus Assay in TK6
Cells (Study Nonclin-0233) and CK-3834283: In Vitro Mammalian Cell Micronucleus
Assay in TK6 Cells (Study Nonclin-0236)


TK6 lymphoblasts were treated with M1a or M1b in the presence and absence of rat liver S9
up to 353 µg/mL for 4 hours, or in the absence of S9 for 27 hours.



A statistically significant increase in micronuclei was observed at M1a concentrations
≥200 µg/mL in the absence of S9 after 27 hours of treatment compared to controls. The value
of the micronuclei induction at the highest dose (1.55%) was only slightly above the
maximum historical control value (1.5%).



Under the conditions of the study, M1a was considered positive for inducing micronuclei
without S9 activation at exposure equivalent to ≥4000-times clinical Cmax at the maximum
recommended human dose.



No statistically significant increases in micronuclei induction were found for M1b under any
condition up to 353 µg/mL in the absence of S9 for 27 hours.

Study Number/Title: CK-3834282/Combined In Vivo Mammalian Peripheral Blood
Erythrocyte Micronucleus Test and Alkaline Comet Assay in Rat (Study Nonclin-0276)


Vehicle, positive controls, or M1a up to 2000 mg/kg/day was administered to rats by oral
gavage for 4 consecutive days after which micronucleated reticulocytes in the periphery were
counted using flow cytometry. The mean percentage of micronucleated cells from M1a
treated animals at all doses was within historical negative control range.



In the same study, hepatocytes were used for a comet assay to assess DNA damage. All M1a
treated groups had Tail DNA values within historical negative control range.



Positive controls induced increases in the mean percentage of micronucleated reticulocytes
and Tail DNA in the comet assay, verifying the validity of the test system.



M1a was not clastogenic in vivo up to 2900-times clinical Cmax and 440-times clinical AUC
at the maximum recommended clinical dose for aficamten. The in vitro plasma protein
binding of M1a was similar between rat and human. Although the major metabolite M1a
without metabolic activation was positive for inducing micronuclei in human lymphoblasts in
vitro, it was not mutagenic in a bacterial Ames assay. M1a administered to rats in vivo for 4
days did not induce micronuclei in circulating reticulocytes and did not cause DNA damage
in hepatocytes at a dose >1000-times the clinical exposure based on Cmax and >400-times
based on AUC, similar to the exposure which did induce micronuclei in lymphoblasts in
vitro. Taken together, the weight of evidence suggests that the M1a metabolite is unlikely to
be mutagenic or clastogenic at clinically relevant doses of aficamten.

Reference ID: 5715605

Results/Conclusion
We concur with the Applicant’s position that CK-3943113 was not mutagenic under any
conditions used for this study. Revertant colony number slightly exceeded the 98% tolerance
threshold for negative control data at 50 µg/plate and 158 µg/plate with S9 for strains TA98 and
TA1537 respectively. However, revertant colony number did not exceed the ≥2-fold increase
cutoff above negative controls for any strain to be considered positive, and there was no dosedependent increase in revertant colonies for any strain.
