Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
binding of aficamten was observed between clinical study samples from subjects with normal
hepatic function and moderate HI (Study CY 6017).
Blood cell partitioning of aficamten and metabolites (CK-3834282 and CK-3834283) was
evaluated in human whole blood at concentrations of 1 and 10μM (Studies Nonclin-0032 and
Nonclin-0201). Whole blood-to-plasma ratio was approximately 0.9, 0.8, and 0.7 across
concentration levels for aficamten, CK-3834282, and CK-3834283, respectively. No apparent
concentration dependency of partitioning was observed.
In Vitro Hepatic Metabolism
Incubation of aficamten (1μM) with human liver microsomes and recombinant CYP isoenzymes
showed that aficamten was metabolized by CYP2D6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 but not
by CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP2C8 (Study Nonclin-0029). Formation of the metabolite CK3834282 was completely inhibited in the presence of a pan-CYP inhibitor (i.e., 1aminobenzotriazole) but not in the presence of individual CYP2D6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and
CYP3A4-specific inhibitors, supporting that aficamten is metabolized by multiple CYP
isoenzymes. The fraction of aficamten metabolized (fm) by CYP2D6 was evaluated using
Hurelhuman™ hepatocyte microliver model and estimated to be 52.1% in pooled hepatocytes.
In Vitro CYP Inhibition and Induction
CYP Inhibition
Aficamten (0.046 to 100μM) exhibited no direct inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8,
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4 (i.e., half-maximal inhibitory concentration [IC50]
>100μM) when evaluated in human liver microsomes (Study Nonclin-0033). Time-dependent
inhibition was not observed for any of these CYP isoforms when incubated with 0.046 to 100μM
aficamten (Study Nonclin-0052). In a follow-up study, aficamten was potentially a weak
metabolism-dependent inhibitor of CYP2C9 because minimal metabolism-dependent inhibition
(i.e., approximately 22%) was observed at the highest test concentration of 50μM; the IC50 of
this inhibition was >50μM (Study Nonclin-0160). The Applicant reports that test concentration
of 50μM is approximately 580 times higher than the maximal unbound plasma concentration of
aficamten at steady-state (Imax,u) of 0.054μM based on a maximal total exposure in humans
(Imax,ss) of 0.972μM occurring with dosing aficamten 20-mg QD (Trial CY 6031) and the human
unbound fraction in plasma (fu,p) of 0.056 (Study CY 6017).
Aficamten metabolites, CK-3834282 and CK-3834283 (0.046 to 100μM) exhibited no direct
inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4midazolam (IC50>100 μM) when evaluated
in human liver microsomes (Study Nonclin-0126). The IC50 of CK-3834282 was >100, 22.2,
88.7, and 76.8μM for CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4testosterone, respectively. The
IC50 of CK-3834283 was 65.3, 19.5, 96.9, and 87.3μM for CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and
CYP3A4testosterone, respectively. Time-dependent inhibition was not observed for CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4 with either metabolite.
CYP Induction
Aficamten (0.3, 1, 3, 10, 25, and 50μM) did not exhibit potential to induce CYP2B6 or CYP3A4
but potentially induced CYP1A2 when evaluated by quantitation of messenger ribonucleic acid
(mRNA) expression in human primary hepatocytes (Study Nonclin-0016). The potential of
120
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
aficamten and its metabolites (CK-3834282 and CK-3834283) for clinically relevant induction of
CYP1A2 was evaluated using the “basic mRNA fold-change method” in accordance with the
ICH guidance for industry M12 Drug Interaction Studies (August 2024). The lowest measured
test concentration that resulted in a ≥2-fold increase in CYP1A2 mRNA expression relative to
vehicle control was approximately 926, 245, and 41 times the Imax,u and Cmax,u for aficamten, CK3834282, and CK-3834283, respectively, at the highest proposed therapeutic dose (aficamten 20mg QD) (Study Nonclin-0494). Therefore, aficamten and its metabolites are not predicted to be
potential in vivo CYP1A2 inducers in humans and a confirmatory clinical DDI study was not
conducted to evaluate this potential.
In Vitro Interaction With Transporters
In vitro, aficamten (0.25 to 25μM) was not a substrate of breast cancer resistance protein
(BCRP), P-gp, organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, or OATP1B3 when
evaluated in Madin-Darby canine kidney-II (MDCK-II) cells (Study Nonclin-0009).
The potential for aficamten and its metabolites (CK-3834282 and CK-3834283) to inhibit efflux
(BCRP and P-gp) and solute-linked carrier (i.e., organic cation transporter [OCT]2, OCT3,
organic anion transporter [OAT]1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, multidrug and toxin extrusion
proteins [MATE]1, and MATE2-K) transporters was evaluated in transporter-transfected
MDCK-II cells (Studies Nonclin-0008, Nonclin-0127, and Nonclin-0169). Aficamten (50μM)
did not significantly inhibit BCRP-, OAT1-, OATP1B3-, OCT2, or OCT3-mediated transport.
Clinically relevant inhibition of OATP1B1, OAT3, MATE1, and MATE2-K by aficamten is
considered unlikely because the R values, calculated as [Imax,u]/IC50 for OCT2, OAT3, MATE1,
and MATE2-K or as 1 + (unbound fraction in plasma [fu,p] × estimated maximum plasma
inhibitor concentration at the inlet to the liver [Iin,max])/IC50) for OATP1B1, determined using the
highest proposed therapeutic dose (aficamten 20-mg QD) were below the thresholds for potential
risk of 0.1 and 1.1, respectively. The ratio for unbound hepatic inlet concentration (Cmax,u,inlet)/Ki
or IC50 (OATP1B1 and OAT3) and Cmax,u/Ki or IC50 (MATE1 and MATE2-K) determined at the
highest therapeutic dose of aficamten 20-mg QD for both cases was below the threshold for
potential risk of 0.1 and 0.02, respectively, per the ICH guidance for industry M12 Drug
Interaction Studies (August 2024).
However, the potential for clinically relevant inhibition of P-gp by aficamten could not be
excluded because the intestinal luminal concentration (calculated as dose/250 mL)/IC50 was 48.8,
which is above the threshold for potential risk of 10. Consequently, a clinical DDI study was
conducted to evaluate the effect of aficamten on the pharmacokinetics of a sensitive P-gp
substrate, dabigatran (Study CY 6014, Cohort 3).
In addition, the Applicant reports that aficamten metabolites, CK-3834282 and CK-383283 are
not a sensitive substrate of drug transporters. CK-3834282 and CK-383283 are not expected to
inhibit BCRP, OCT2, OCT3, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, or MATE2-K drug
transporters at clinically relevant concentrations.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Study CY 6011: Single- and Multiple-Dose
Pharmacokinetics in Healthy Subjects and SingleDose Pharmacokinetics in CYP2D6 Poor
Metabolizers
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, multipart, single- and
multiple-ascending dose (MAD) study to determine the safety and tolerability of aficamten in
healthy subjects. This study was conducted in 5 parts.
Part 1 (SAD) and Part 2 (MAD)
Parts 1 and 2 used a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled design to evaluate the PK
profile of aficamten following single-ascending doses (SADs) and MADs and to describe the
PK/PD relationship between aficamten and cardiac function. In Part 1, a total of 57 subjects were
randomized to receive a single oral dose of aficamten at ascending dose levels of 1, 3, 10, 25, 40,
50, and 75 mg capsules (n=42) or matching placebo (n=15). All 57 subjects completed Part 1. In
Part 2, a total of 24 subjects were randomized to receive oral aficamten QD at ascending dose
levels of 5, 7.5, and 10 mg capsules (n=18) or matching placebo (n=6) for 14 or 17 days. All 24
subjects completed Part 2.
Part 3 (CYP2D6 PM Cohort)
CYP2D6 genotypes were determined at Screening. Subjects identified as CYP2D6 PMs were
excluded from the SAD (i.e., Part 1) and MAD (i.e., Part 2) cohorts and instead invited to
participate in the separate CYP2D6 PM cohort (i.e., Part 3). A total of 33 subjects had CYP2D6
genotype and phenotype determined: 7 PMs (i.e., *3/*4, *4/*4, *4/*5, or *4/*12), 5 intermediate
metabolizers (IMs) (i.e., *1/*4, *2A/*9, or *2A/unknown), and 21 normal metabolizers (NMs)
(i.e., *1/*1, *1/*2, *1/*2A, *1/*10, *1/*41, *2/*2, *2A/*2A, *2A/*41, or *2AxN/*3) were
identified. Part 3 used a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single-dose design to
assess the effect of CYP2D6 genetic variants on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten. In Part 3, a
total of 9 healthy CYP2D6-PMs were randomized to receive a single oral dose of aficamten
10 mg encapsulated granules capsule (n=7) or matching placebo (n=2). All 9 subjects completed
Part 3.
Part 4 (Food Effect) and Part 5 (Relative Bioavailability)
Both parts 4 and 5 used an open-label, randomized, 2-way crossover design to evaluate the effect
of a high-fat meal on the single-dose pharmacokinetics of aficamten (i.e., 1×10 mg encapsulated
granules 5% [w/w] capsule) and the relative bioavailability between the initial clinical
formulation (i.e., 2×5 mg encapsulated granules 5% [w/w] capsules) and the Phase 2 tablets (i.e.,
2×5 mg 5% [w/w] blend tablets) formulation in healthy subjects, respectively For Part 5,
treatments were given in the fasted state and separated by a ≥14-day washout period. A total of
12 subjects were randomized and completed Part 5.
122
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
The food effect cohort (i.e., Part 4) in this study was assessed using a 10-mg capsule formulation
while Study CY 6012 was conducted using a 20 mg TBM tablet formulation. Therefore, Study
CY 6012 will be used to assess the food effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics and Part 4 of this
study will not be covered in this review.
Study Results
Part 1 (SAD)
Plasma aficamten AUC24, AUC∞, and Cmax appeared to increase proportional to dose following
single oral doses of 1, 3, 10, 25, 40, 50, and 75-mg aficamten (Figure 14 and Table 78). Median
t½ was consistent over the dose range (i.e., 75.2 to 81.6 hours).
Part 2 (MAD)
Plasma aficamten AUC24 and Cmax approximately doubled with twice the dose following single
oral doses of 5 and 10-mg aficamten (Figure 15 and Table 79). Similar results were observed
following multiple-dose administration for 14 days. However, AUCs observed with 7.5-mg
aficamten were comparable to those from 10-mg aficamten. The Applicant states that
comparable exposures observed between 7.5 and 10-mg QD dosing may be due to the small
sample size and variability.

14.2.2. Study CY 6012: Food Effect of the To-BeMarketed Formulation in Healthy Volunteers
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, open-label, randomized, 2-way crossover study to evaluate the effect of a
high-fat meal on the single-dose pharmacokinetics of aficamten following administration as the
proposed TBM tablet formulation in healthy subjects. A total of 18 subjects were randomized to
treatment order, received a single oral dose of 20-mg aficamten (i.e., 1×20 mg 10% [w/w] blend
tablet) under fasted and fed conditions, and completed the study. Treatments were separated by a
≥20-day washout period.
Study Results
Administration with a high-fat meal altered the rate but not the extent of absorption of aficamten,
as indicated by the comparable total plasma exposure (i.e., AUCt and AUC∞) observed when
aficamten was administered with and without food and the 15% lower Cmax (i.e., geometric mean
129

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
ratio 90% CI: 72 to 101%) and delayed Tmax (i.e., median: 2.19 versus 0.60 hours postdose)
observed under fed condition (Table 84 and Table 85).

14.2.3. Study CY 6014: DDI Potential With CYP3A
Inhibitors, CYP Inducers, and P-gp Substrates in
Healthy Volunteers
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, open-label, non-randomized, fixed-sequence, DDI study to assess the effect
of itraconazole (i.e., strong CYP3A inhibitor) and carbamazepine (i.e., CYP inducer) on the
pharmacokinetics of aficamten and to assess the effect of aficamten on the pharmacokinetics of
dabigatran (i.e., administered as dabigatran etexilate, a sensitive P-gp probe) in healthy subjects.
A total of 59 subjects were enrolled to receive the following treatments:
Cohort 1 (Itraconazole)
Single oral dose of aficamten 10 mg (i.e., 2 × 5-mg tablets) given 1 hour after a standardized
breakfast on Day 1 and 1 hour after the itraconazole dose on Day 13. Itraconazole 200-mg QD
on Days 10 to 21 given within 5 minutes of a standardized breakfast (n=17)
Cohort 2 (Carbamazepine)
Single oral dose of aficamten 20 mg (i.e., 4 × 5-mg tablets) within 5 minutes of a standardized
breakfast on Day 1 and with the morning dose of carbamazepine on Day 28. Oral carbamazepine
100 mg twice daily (BID) on Days 10 to 12, 200-mg BID on Days 13 to 16, and 300-mg BID on
Days 17 to 36 given within 5 minutes of a standardized meal (n=17)
Cohort 3 (Dabigatran)
Single oral dose of dabigatran etexilate 75 mg within 5 minutes of a standardized breakfast on
Day 1 and a single oral dose of dabigatran etexilate 75 mg coadministered with a single oral dose
of aficamten 20 mg (i.e., 4 × 5-mg tablets) given within 5 minutes of a standardized breakfast on
Day 3 (n=25)
Study Results
Cohort 1
Seventeen subjects were enrolled and dosed, and all subjects completed the study in accordance
with the protocol. A small increase in plasma aficamten AUC∞ was observed upon
coadministration of aficamten with itraconazole (i.e., a strong CYP3A inhibitor) in both analysis
of the entire PK population and sensitivity analysis excluding one subject with an unexplained
decrease in exposures upon itraconazole coadministration (Table 86). The Applicant concludes
that minimal increase in aficamten AUC∞ (i.e., approximately 26%) and lack of change in CK3834282 and CK-3834283 metabolite-to-parent ratios observed upon itraconazole
coadministration (Table 87) indicate that CYP3A does not contribute significantly to the
formation of circulating metabolites and is responsible for only approximately 20% of aficamten
elimination. As such, the Applicant believes that aficamten may be coadministered with CYP3A
inhibitors without dose adjustment.

14.2.4. Study CY 601-10: DDI Potential With CYP3A,
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 Inhibitors in
Healthy Volunteers
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, open-label, non-randomized, fixed-sequence, DDI study to evaluate the
effect of fluconazole (i.e., strong inhibitor of CYP2C19 and moderate inhibitor of CYP3A and
CYP2C9), paroxetine (i.e., strong selective inhibitor of CYP2D6), and fluoxetine (i.e., strong
inhibitor of CYP2C19 and CYP2D6) on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten in healthy subjects.
A total of 51 subjects were enrolled to receive the following treatments:

137
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Cohort 1 (Fluconazole)
Single oral dose of aficamten 10 mg (2×5-mg tablets) given 1.5 hours after a standardized
breakfast on Day 1 and 1 hour after fluconazole dose on Day 19. Fluconazole 400-mg QD on
Days 10 to 27 given 30 minutes after a standardized breakfast (n=17).
Cohort 2 (Paroxetine)
Single oral dose of aficamten 10 mg (2×5-mg tablets) given 1.5 hours after a standardized
breakfast on Day 1 and 1 hour after paroxetine dose on Day 26. Paroxetine 20-mg QD on Days
10 to 16 and 40-mg QD on Days 17 to 34 given 30 minutes after a standardized breakfast (n=17).
Cohort 3 (Fluoxetine)
Single oral dose of aficamten 10 mg (2×5-mg tablets) given 1.5 hours after a standardized
breakfast on Day 1 and 1 hour after fluoxetine dose on Day 28. Fluoxetine 20-mg QD on Days
10 to 16 and 40-mg QD on Days 17 to 36 given 30 minutes after a standardized breakfast (n=17).
A washout period of 9 days separated aficamten dosing on Day 1 and the first dose of
fluconazole, paroxetine, or fluoxetine.
In Cohorts 1 (fluconazole) and 3 (fluoxetine), subjects who were identified as CYP2C9 and
CYP2C19 extensive (i.e., normal), rapid, or ultra-rapid metabolizers were enrolled while for
Cohort 2 (paroxetine), subjects who were identified as CYP2D6 extensive (i.e., normal), rapid,
or ultra-rapid metabolizers were enrolled.
The Applicant states that for Cohort 3, a 40-mg dose of fluoxetine was considered the highest
clinical dose that could be administered safely in healthy subjects for 20 days. A survey of the
literature indicated that maximum inhibition of CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 by fluoxetine is
achieved at this dose (Greenblatt et al. 1992; Jeppesen et al. 1996; Harvey and Preskorn 2001).
The effect of fluoxetine 60 mg, the approved maximum daily dose, was evaluated in PBPK
modeling.
Study Results
Cohort 1
A total of 17 subjects entered Cohort 1 of the study and received at least one dose of study drug
according to the protocol. A total of 16 subjects completed the study. One subject (i.e., 60110001004- (b) (6) ) withdrew from the study on Day 14 due to a personal reason (i.e., death in the
family). Coadministration of aficamten with fluconazole led to an approximately 278% increase
in plasma aficamten AUC∞ with no change in Cmax (Table 93). A prolongation of aficamten t½
(~3-fold) was also observed, suggesting inhibition of systemic clearance of aficamten by
fluconazole, rather than an increase in aficamten bioavailability. Considering the magnitude of
contribution of CYP3A to aficamten elimination observed in Study CY 6014 (i.e., increase in
aficamten AUC∞ of approximately 26% with coadministration with itraconazole), it was
assumed that the increase in aficamten exposure in the presence of fluconazole was mediated
primarily by inhibition of CYP2C9 and/or CYP2C19. The Applicant proposes to contraindicate
the concomitant administration of aficamten with fluconazole when taken for more than 3 days.
See Section 8.2 for the Clinical Pharmacology review team’s dosing recommendations.

Across all cohorts, CK-3834282 and CK-3834283 metabolite ratios (MRAUC∞) decreased upon

confirm that all evaluated DDI precipitant drugs act by altering the systemic CYP-mediated
metabolism of aficamten.
Based on the study outcome of Cohort 3 in which fluoxetine increased the AUC0-inf of aficamten
by 32%, comparable to the increase in aficamten exposure in the paroxetine cohort (27%), the
Applicant concludes that CYP2C19 plays a minor role in aficamten elimination.
Taken together, the Applicant believes that aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9mediated metabolism with contributions from CYP3A (inferred from CY 6014), and relatively
smaller contributions for metabolism via CYP2C19 and CYP2D6.
The Applicant’s proposal of allowing coadministration of aficamten with CYP2D6 and
CYP2C19 inhibitors is reasonable.

14.2.5. Study CY 6017: Effect of Hepatic Impairment
on Aficamten Pharmacokinetics
Study Design
This was a Phase 1, open-label, non-randomized, 2-part, single-dose, parallel-design study to
evaluate the pharmacokinetics of aficamten in subjects with HI compared to subjects with
normal hepatic function. In Part 1, a total of 8 subjects with moderate HI (i.e., Group A) and 8
healthy subjects (i.e., matched controls; Group B) were enrolled and received a single oral dose
of aficamten 20 mg (i.e., 4×5 mg Phase 3, 10% [w/w] blend tablets). The effect of mild HI was
planned to be assessed in Part 2 but was not conducted based on review of the pharmacokinetics
and safety assessment data from Part 1.
142
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Study Results
Plasma aficamten AUC was similar between subjects with moderate HI (i.e., Child-Pugh score of
≥7 and ≤9) and normal hepatic function, while Cmax was moderately (38%) higher in subjects
with moderate HI relative to those with normal hepatic function (Table 98 and Table 99). There
were no statistically significant differences in aficamten t½ (p =0.72) and apparent clearance
(CL/F) (p =1.00) between subjects with moderate HI and normal hepatic function (Study CY
6017 CSR Table 11 to 5).

Results from this study indicate no clinically relevant changes in aficamten pharmacokinetics in
subjects with moderate HI versus matched control subjects with normal hepatic function. These
findings can be extrapolated to subjects with mild HI, suggesting that no dose adjustment of
aficamten is warranted in subjects with mild or moderate HI.

14.2.6. Study CY 6013: Mass Balance
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, nonrandomized, single-dose study to determine the mass balance
of 14C-aficamten following oral administration; to characterize the pharmacokinetics of
aficamten and its metabolites, CK-3834282 and CK-3834283, in plasma and urine; and to
investigate the metabolite profile of 14C-aficamten in plasma, urine, and feces in healthy male
subjects. A total of 8 subjects were enrolled, received a single oral dose of a 20 mg capsule
containing approximately 100 μCi of 14C-aficamten, and completed the study. All subjects fasted
overnight for at least 10 hours and refrained from consuming water for 1 hour predose and 2
hours postdose. Subjects fasted until approximately 4 hours postdose.
Study Results
Following administration of 14C-aficamten 20 mg, absorption of aficamten was moderate and
formation of metabolites, M1a (CK-3834282) and M1b (CK-3834283) was steady with Cmax
observed 3 hours later than parent (median Tmax: 5.00 versus 2.00 hours postdose). The median
t½ of aficamten was 99.6 hours and similar t½ were observed for the metabolites (i.e., 94.8 and
95.4 hours), suggesting that M1a (CK-3834282) and M1b (CK-3834283) elimination is
formation rate-limited (Table 100).

144
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Table 100. Summary of Plasma PK Parameters for Aficamten, M1a (CK-3834282), M1b (CK3834283), and Total Radioactivity Following a Single Oral Dose of 20 mg 14C-Aficamten in Healthy
Male Subjects, Study CY 6013

Source: Table 7, Module 2.7.2
Abbreviations: M, metabolite; PK, pharmacokinetic

At 600 hours postdose, the overall mean recovery of total radioactivity was 89.7% (range: 76.4%
to 98.1%). Feces was the primary route of excretion and accounted for 57.7% of total
radioactivity recovered, while urine accounted for 32.0% of total radioactivity recovered. Urine
recovery of aficamten and metabolites, M1a (CK-3834282) and M1b (CK-3834283), was low,
representing 0.06%, 5.3%, and 2.3%, respectively, of the administered dose. Corresponding renal
clearance was 2.6, 443, and 105 mL/h, respectively.
Aficamten was primarily eliminated by metabolism and, to a lesser extent, by fecal excretion as
unchanged aficamten (5.1%). Metabolism was mediated primarily by oxidation and reduction,
with secondary pathways of glucuronidation, hydrolysis, and sulfonation (Figure 16). In plasma,
the most abundant circulating components were aficamten, M1a (CK-3834282), M1b (CK3834283), and M5 (CK-4034051), which accounted for 19.8%, 10.5%, 36.4%, and 10.3%,
respectively, of total circulating radioactivity. Urinary metabolites were generally minor in
abundance (i.e., <10% of dose) and the only major fecal metabolite identified was M18 (CK4017583), which accounted for 44.1% of dose.

14.2.7. Study CY 6019: Single-Dose Pharmacokinetics
and Effect on QTc Interval in Healthy Subjects
Study Design
This was a randomized, double-blind, positive- and placebo-controlled, 3-way crossover design
to evaluate effect of aficamten and its metabolites (i.e., CK-3834282 and CK-3834283) on the
corrected value of the interval between the Q- and T-waves on the electrocardiogram tracing
(QTc) interval. Subjects were randomized to treatment order and received a single oral dose of
aficamten 50 mg (i.e., 10×5-mg aficamten tablets), placebo for aficamten, and 400-mg
moxifloxacin (i.e., 1×400-mg tablet) separately in three treatment periods. Treatments were
separated by a ≥21-day washout period.
Study Results
Reference is made to the QT Study Review from the Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac
Safety Studies dated July 22, 2024 (Reference ID: 5412605) in DARRTS. The Interdisciplinary
Review Team concluded that aficamten did not cause a clinically meaningful change in the QTc
interval following a single 50-mg dose.

14.2.8. Study CY 6021: Dose Finding
Study Design
This was a Phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-finding
study in subjects with HCM with the following key objectives: (1) to determine the safety and
146
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
tolerability of aficamten; (2) to describe the concentration- and dose-response relationship of
aficamten on the resting and Valsalva LVOT-G over 10 weeks of treatment; (3) to evaluate the
concentration-response relationship of aficamten on resting LVEF over 10 weeks of treatment;
(4) to describe the PK/PD relationships of aficamten; and (5) to assess the effect of background
HCM therapy on the PK and PD response of aficamten.
The clinical development program sought to maintain aficamten plasma concentrations required
for efficacy, but with the understanding that supratherapeutic concentrations may result in
systolic dysfunction, leading to decreases in stroke volume and cardiac output, compensatory
increases in heart rate, and potentially clinical heart failure. Therefore, the goal of dose selection
was to find the lowest possible dose to safely achieve resting and Valsalva LVOT-G targets (i.e.,
resting LVOT-G of <30 mm Hg and Valsalva LVOT-G of <50 mm Hg) and maintain normal
LVEF (i.e., LVEF ≥50%).
Doses for the Phase 3 study were selected based on the results from subjects with oHCM
enrolled in Cohorts 1 and 2 of the study and therefore, this review focuses on Cohorts 1 and 2.
Cohorts 1 and 2 enrolled subjects with oHCM on background therapy including beta-blockers
and/or calcium channel blockers, either as monotherapy or combined, but excluding the use of
disopyramide. Subjects with symptomatic oHCM were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to aficamten or
placebo and received up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (i.e., 5, 10, and 15 mg in Cohort 1 and
10, 20, and 30 mg in Cohort 2) or placebo once daily for 10 weeks each guided by
echocardiographic results. The dose titration criteria based on echocardiograms used in this study
is summarized in Table 101.
Table 101. Echocardiogram Criteria for Scheduled Dose Titrations, Study CY 6021, Cohorts 1 and
2

Source: Table 4, Module 2.7.3

There was a 4-week post-treatment follow-up period following the active treatment period.
Sparse sampling for pharmacokinetics was performed in this study, and the PK data collected
were used to inform the Population PK (PPK) analysis.
Study Results
The 5 mg starting dose was PD effective (i.e., had responders achieving resting and Valsalva
LVOT-G targets and maintaining normal LVEF) and well-tolerated by all subjects. There were
11 out of 41 subjects in Cohorts 1 and 2 who required doses higher than 10 mg to achieve target
LVOT-G (Table 8). In Cohort 1, 5 subjects were escalated to the 15-mg dose. In Cohort 2, 6
147
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
subjects were escalated to the 20-mg dose; 4 subjects achieved target gradients at this dose level
(although 1 subject was down-titrated to 10 mg due to LVEF <50% per site-read
echocardiography), while 2 subjects were escalated further to 30 mg (Table 8). One of the 2
subjects on the 30-mg dose was down-titrated to 20 mg due to having a LVEF of 42% at Week 6
(per site-read echocardiography), and this subject also had a temporal treatment-emergent
adverse event (TEAE) of peripheral edema (of moderate severity). The Applicant believes that
data suggested that the 30-mg dose is not likely to add substantial clinical benefit versus risk;
therefore, 20 mg was selected as the highest dose in the Phase 3 study. The Applicant’s dose
selection for the Phase 3 study appears to be reasonable.

14.2.9. Trial CY 6031: Dose Proportionality and
Phase 3 Trial Formulation
The Phase 3 Trial (CY 6031) was conducted using multiple units of 5-mg aficamten tablets
instead of the TBM formulation of 10 mg, 15 mg, and 20-mg aficamten tablets. Therefore, the
establishment of dose proportionality across the proposed TBM dose range is important.
The dose proportionality assessment of multiple QD doses of aficamten was limited to 5 mg to
10 mg in healthy subjects (Study CY 6011, Part 2) due to the limit of tolerability of LVEF
reduction in this population. On April 18, 2025 (SDN: 030), the Applicant submitted the
information below describing their assessment of dose proportionality for doses of 15-mg QD
and 20-mg QD.
The Applicant has examined dose proportionality of aficamten across the therapeutic dose range
(5-mg QD to 20-mg QD) in the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) in subjects with oHCM using PPK
analysis. A two-compartment model with linear absorption and elimination was used to describe
the pharmacokinetics of aficamten. The summary of the post hoc PK estimates at different dose
levels where all subjects were given the same dose of aficamten is shown in Table 102. Results
demonstrate dose proportionality up to the highest clinical dose of 20-mg QD.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Summary of Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
A PPK analysis was conducted to support the dose selection for the treatment of adults with
symptomatic oHCM.

177
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
The objectives of the Applicant’s modeling analysis were:


To develop a PPK model from the pooled PK data in healthy subjects and subjects with
HCM treated with aficamten.



To identify the intrinsic and extrinsic factors impacting aficamten pharmacokinetics and
assess the magnitude of covariates with significant effect.



To provide individual PK parameters of aficamten for planned subsequent PK/PD and E-R
analyses.

Methods
Pooled data from Phase 1 to 3 studies including healthy subjects and subjects with oHCM after
oral administration of aficamten were included in the analysis. Population PK modeling was
performed with the first-order conditional estimation with etaepsilon interaction (FOCE-I)
method in NONMEM® Version 7.5.0. Model covariates were selected through univariate
screening followed by a stepwise backward elimination method based on statistical significance
levels as of 0.01 and 0.001, respectively. Model evaluations were performed by inspection of
model parameters and their confidence intervals, standard residual-based goodness-of-fit plots,
and simulation-based diagnostics such as prediction corrected visual predictive checks. The
impact of covariates on the PK exposures in subjects with oHCM was evaluated using boxplots
and summary tables. After the model validation, the final PPK model was used to calculate the
post hoc PK exposure metrics needed for subjects with oHCM and support for the subsequent ER analyses.
Results
The PPK dataset included 9963 PK samples from 447 subjects, including 264 healthy subjects
and 183 subjects with oHCM. Table 109 summarizes the clinical studies included in this
analysis. A two-compartment model with linear elimination was used to describe the
pharmacokinetics of aficamten. Absorption of aficamten was modeled as 1st-order after a lag
time. Among the evaluated covariates, body weight and sex had an impact on both the volume
and clearance of aficamten. Other model-determined significant covariates (i.e., formulation and
food status) demonstrated no-to-minimal impact on the PK exposure. The final PPK model
parameter estimates are provided in Table 110. The goodness-of-fit plots for the final PPK model
are presented in Figure 17. There is good agreement between the individual or population
predicted aficamten concentrations and the observed concentrations. Prediction corrected visual
predictive checks (Figure 18) show that the model adequately captures the central tendency and
range of the PK results observed for all the studies. The final PPK model was used to conduct the
simulations to evaluate the impact of selected statistically significant covariates on the PK
exposures (AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau) in subjects with oHCM given the 15-mg QD regimen at
steady state. The results show that there was no impact of significant covariates on the overall
PK variability in subjects with oHCM. No dose adjustments based on demographics is warranted
since aficamten dose is titrated by echocardiography assessments to achieve desired efficacy
(Figure 19).

The Applicant’s PPK model appears to describe the aficamten serum concentration-time profiles
adequately following the administration of aficamten in subjects with oHCM. Therefore, the
PPK model is acceptable for simulating post hoc exposure metrics, e.g., AUCtau, Cmax, or Ctau of
aficamten for PK/PD and E-R analyses of efficacy or safety measurements.
Covariate analysis identified body weight (47 to 156 kg), population type (i.e., healthy versus
HCM subjects) and sex (39.4% female) as statistically significant covariates on aficamten
pharmacokinetics. The impact of food on the PK exposure of aficamten is minor.

14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
The Applicant described the population PK/PD and conducted the E-R analysis of data from
subjects with oHCM in the Phase 2 (Study CY 6021) and the Phase 3 (Trial CY 6031) programs
to support the proposed commercial aficamten dose titration.

181
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
The objectives of the PK/PD and E-R analyses were:


To develop PK/PD models characterizing longitudinal LVEF and LVOT-G change following
aficamten treatment in subjects with oHCM in Phase 2 and 3 studies.



To evaluate the proposed commercial aficamten dose titration regimen based on less frequent
measurement of LVEF and LVOT-G, using PK/PD simulation.



To quantify the E-R relationship for the primary, secondary, and key exploratory efficacy
endpoints in subjects with oHCM in the Phase 3 study.



To explore the E-R relationship for key safety endpoints in subjects with oHCM in Phase 3
study.

Methods
PD data collected in Study CY 6021 and Trial CY 6031 were used for PK/PD analyses. Efficacy
and safety data collected in Trial CY 6031 were used for E-R analyses. PK/PD analysis was
performed for LVEF and LVOT-G, based on core and site-read laboratory measurements
collected in Studies CY 6021 and CY 6031. The selected exposure metric was the PPK model
predicted average aficamten concentration 24 hr prior to the assessment (Cavg,24). To support the
proposed commercial dose regimen, simulations explored alternate dose titration frequencies
over three years, with the same criteria for dose titration used in the Phase 3 study. The alternate
dose titration regimens included once every two-week (Q2W), once every four-week (Q4W),
once every six-week (Q6W), and once every eight-week (Q8W) dose titrations with a starting
dose of 5-mg QD and dose escalation options of 10 mg to 20-mg QD. Exposure-efficacy analysis
was performed with the selected primary endpoint, which was the change in pVO2 by CPET
from baseline to Week 24. Exposure-safety analyses included incidence of selected TEAEs as
the endpoints. Exposure metrics included in the E-R analyses were the PPK model predicted
Cavg,24 (i.e., Cavg,ss at Week 12 and Week 24 in the exposure-efficacy analysis), model predicted
average aficamten concentration from Day 1 to the time of the event (Cavg,TOE), model predicted
trough aficamten concentration at steady-state (Cmin,ss), and model predicted maximum aficamten
concentration at steady-state (Cmax,ss).
Results
Table 111 summarizes the 2880 LVOT-G and 2876 LVEF data records collected from 183
subjects for the PK/PD analysis. Figure 20 shows the PK/PD relationship for both core and siteread measurements of post Valsalva LVOT-G as well as a declining trend with increasing
exposure observed between the PK exposure metrics and the LVEF measurements.

Exposure-Efficacy Relationship
The Exposure-efficacy population included 282 subjects, of which 140 received placebo and 142
received aficamten. The key findings from the Applicant’s or reviewer’s E-R analyses are as
follows:
1. pVO2 Uptake: in 263 placebo- and aficamten-treated subjects, higher aficamten Cavg,ss was
associated (Spearman coefficient: 0.31) with greater improvements in pVO2 from baseline at
Week 24 (Figure 22).
2. NT-proBNP: in 266 placebo- and aficamten-treated subjects, there was a significant exposure
response relationship (p<0.05) for probability of NT-proBNP change from baseline to Week
24, both with or without placebo.
3. KCCQ-CSS: in 117 placebo- and aficamten-treated subjects, increased response at Week 24
was associated with higer aficamten Cavg,ss (p<0.05) (Figure 22).
4. ≥1 NYHA Class Improvement: in 117 placebo- and aficamten-treated subjects, there was
also a significant exposure response relationship (p<0.05) for probability of change from
baseline to Week 24, both with or without placebo (Figure 22).
5. hs-cardiac-TnI: While large variability in changes in hs-cardiac-TnI from baseline was found,
a relationship between this endpoint and aficamten exposure was not detected (Figure 23).

Exposure-Safety Analyses
The same population (282 subjects from Trial CY 6031, of which 140 received placebo and 142
received aficamten) was used in the exposure-safety analysis. A boxplot comparison (Figure 24)
indicates that the rate of TEAEs was similar between placebo and aficamten-treated subjects,
which included ICD discharge, persistent AF, major adverse cardiovascular event, Grade ≥2
cardiovascular AE, any Grade cardiovascular AE, headache, hypertension, palpitations, and
upper respiratory tract infection as safety endpoints. Table 114 shows that the rate of TEAEs did
not increase with increasing aficamten exposures in the aficamten-treated group.



The PK/PD model of aficamten is adequate to link aficamten exposure to LVEF and LVOTG for aficamten-treated subjects from Study CY 6021 and Study 6031. The results of this
analysis were used to support the proposed commercial dose regimen.



The Pharmacometrics team has conducted independent analyses to evaluate the frequency of
echocardiographic assessment during the titration and maintenance phases. Please refer to
Section 6.1.



Exposure-efficacy analyses demonstrated statistically significant relationships (p<0.05)
between PK exposures and several efficacy endpoints, including the probability of ≥1 NYHA
class improvement at Week 24, KCCQ-CSS change at Week 24, and NT-proBNP change at
Week 24. However, exposure-efficacy analyses for pVO2 change and hs-cardiac-TnI change
at Week 24 did not demonstrate significant exposure-response relationships in the aficamtentreated group. The results of exposure-safety analyses did not show any trend between
aficamten exposure (Cavg,ss and Cmax,ss) and the evaluated safety endpoints.

14.5.3. Physiologically-Based PK Modeling
14.5.3.1. Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s PBPK analyses of
predicting the DDI potential of aficamten as an object of strong, moderate and weak inhibitors or
inducers of CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and/or CYP3A in CYP2C9 normal and poor
metabolizers (NM and PM).
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK analyses reports (cyt-11-b-report), the
response to FDA’s information requests submitted on February 24 (seq 0017), March 10, 2025
(seq 0021), and April 1, 2025 (seq 0026), and the modeling supporting files, and concluded as
below:


The strong CYP inducer rifampin was predicted to decrease aficamten AUC up to 87%.



A moderate CYP inducer was predicted to decrease aficamten AUC up to 46%.



A strong or moderate inhibitor of CYP3A or CYP2D6 was predicted to increase aficamten
AUC up to 1.5-fold.



The strong CYP2C9 inhibitor sulfaphenazole was predicted to increase aficamten AUC up to
2.6-fold.



The prediction results for CYP inhibitors targeting multiple metabolic pathways involved in
aficamten metabolism are complicated by the uncertainties regarding the contribution of
CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 to aficamten metabolism. The effects of these inhibitors on
aficamten exposure were predicted using an aficamten model without or with 3% or 21%
contribution of CYP2D6 in aficamten metabolism incorporated, as summarized in Table 124.
14.5.3.2. Background

Refer to Sections 5.2, 8.2, 14.1, and 14.2 for detailed information on aficamten regarding ADME
properties, in vitro and clinical studies that was used in PBPK modeling.
14.5.3.3. Methods
Simulations related to evaluation of aficamten as an object of CYP inhibitors and inducers were
performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator (Version 22, Certara,
Sheffield, UK). Schemes of the PBPK simulation strategy are shown in Figure 25, which
summarizes the studies used for aficamten model development and verification, and model
applications in DDI predictions. The final model input parameters are presented in Table 115.
The aficamten PBPK model consists of a first-order absorption model, a minimal PBPK model
with a single adjusting compartment for distribution, and an enzyme kinetics model for
elimination. The Simcyp library files itraconazole_Fed Capsule, itraconazole_Fasted soln,
fluconazole, fluvoxamine, paroxetine, sulphaphenazole, verapamil, duloxetine, and voriconazole
were used for DDI simulations without any modification. For the fluoxetine model, CYP3A4
inhibition parameters were excluded.
192
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
Additional Comments
6. The Applicant simulated interactions with carbamazepine using an updated carbamazepine
model. In the updated model, CYP2C9 Indmax and IndC50 values of 4.19 and 41.2 mM were
incorporated in the model (Endo-Tsukude et al. 2019), and the Indslope of CYP3A4 and
CYP3A5 in the original model were replaced with their corresponding Indmax and IndC50
values of 8.27 and 16 mM for CYP3A4 and 2.95 and 142 mM for CYP3A5 (Stresser et al.
2016). No changes were made to the model of its metabolite SV-Carbamazepine-10,11epoxide. The performance of this updated carbamazepine model in predicting its induction
effects on CYP3A substrates is presented in Table 116. Since the CYP induction parameters
in the carbamazepine model were updated specifically for this application, the Applicant was
requested to provide additional verification of CYP2C9 induction by simulating the
carbamazepine DDIs with S-warfarin and seladelpar,6 and the results are shown in Table 117,
Table 118, and Figure 26.
7. The rifampin-MD from version 23 was used for rifampin-related simulations. Rifampin 15mg QD was used to simulate the effects of moderate CYP inducers on aficamten because of
the lack of induction parameters of CYP2C19 and CYP2C9 in the commonly used efavirenz
model. The Applicant was requested to provide evidence that rifampin 15-mg QD behaves as
a moderate CYP3A and CYP2C19 and weak CYP2C9 inducer. The results are shown in
Table 119.
8. Upon the Agency’s request, the Applicant provided the verification data for populations with
CYP2D6 NM and CYP2D6 PM and populations with CYP2C9 NM and CYP2C9 PM. For
the population models with different CYP2D6 phenotypes (Figure 27), although the majority
of the AUC values of CYP2D6 substrates were predicted within 2-fold of the clinical
observations, the predicted AUC differences between CYP2D6 NM and PM fell outside the
0.8 and 1.25. For the population models with different CYP2C9 phenotypes (Figure 28), the
models could generally capture the observed plasma concentration-time profiles of Swarfarin and tolbutamide.
9. Fluconazole DDI with aficamten was used to verify the contribution of CYP3A, CYP2C9
and CYP2C19. The ability of the fluconazole model to predict its effects on substrates of
these enzymes was examined and the results are shown in Table 120.
10. Paroxetine was a reversible and time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4 in vitro. Although the
CYP3A inhibition parameters were incorporated into the paroxetine model, it did not predict
its interaction with quinidine, a moderately sensitive CYP3A substrate (Table 121). The
reviewer updated the model by reducing the nonspecific binding parameter related to the
time-dependent inhibition (fu,mic) from 1 to 0.225 (Of note, similar optimization was done for
the time-dependent inhibition parameters of CYP2D6 generated under similar experimental
conditions [Simcyp paroxetine compound summary]). The modified model successfully
reproduced the interaction between paroxetine and quinidine (Table 121) and thus was
utilized in the subsequent analysis by the reviewer. Further details are provided in
Section 14.5.3.4. Of note, this modified paroxetine model predicted that paroxetine did not
significantly increase the exposure of other CYP3A substrates (Table 121), which is
consistent with clinical observations. In addition, the CYP2D6 time-dependent inhibition
parameters from the same publication as the CYP3A inhibition parameters were reduced to

14.5.3.4. Results
Can the PBPK Model Adequately Describe the PK Profiles of Aficamten?
The aficamten PBPK model simulated plasma concentration-time profiles and PK parameters
following administration of single and multiple doses of aficamten in healthy subjects are shown
in Figure 29, Figure 30, Table 122, and Table 123. The model overpredicted the mean plasma
concentration-time profiles compared to the observed mean profiles, but the predicted geometric
mean PK parameters were mostly within the 0.8 to 1.25 bounds of the observed values. The
discrepancy between the mean simulated and mean observed profiles may be attributed to
200
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
relatively large variability in the simulated trials. However, this may not be the case for
aficamten pharmacokinetics in CYP2D6 PMs, where the variability in aficamten Cmax and AUC
between the simulated and the observed values is comparable (Table 123). The overprediction of
aficamten pharmacokinetics in subjects with CYP2D6 PM will be further discussed later.

Can PBPK Analyses Predict the Effects of CYP Inhibitors and Inducers on the
Pharmacokinetics of Aficamten?
The prediction results were complicated by the uncertainties about the contribution of CYP2D6
and CYP2C19 to aficamten metabolism. Table 124 summarizes the predicted effects of both
CYP inhibitors and inducers on the exposure of aficamten following single- or multiple-dose
administration of aficamten, using an aficamten model with or without CYP2D6 metabolism
incorporated. Similar effects of these precipitants on steady-state exposure of aficamten were
predicted (data not shown, Response to information request (seq0021)). A detailed discussion
follows.

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
It should be noted that:
1. Contribution of each CYP in aficamten metabolism was a critical determinant of prediction
accuracy. In vitro identification of CYP enzymes responsible for aficamten metabolism was
conducted using recombinant CYP enzymes and chemical inhibition methods. Aficamten
exhibited low turnover, with minimal changes in aficamten depletion except when incubated
with recombinant CYP2D6. Notably, the major metabolite M1 formation occurred via
recombinant CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and CYP3A (Figure 32). However, isoformselective inhibition of any single enzyme did not completely inhibit M1 formation (Figure
32).
2. The initial fm values of CYP3A, CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 were determined using clinical DDI
studies with itraconazole (Study CY 6014, cohort 1), paroxetine (Study CY 601-10, cohort 2)
and fluoxetine (Study CY 601-10, cohort 3), respectively. The remaining metabolism was
attributable to CYP2C9, based on the assumption that CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 and
CYP3A collectively mediate all aficamten metabolism and that strong CYP inhibitors
completely inhibit the corresponding CYP-mediated metabolism. For CYP2C19, its fm value
was derived by the difference between fm values obtained from the DDI studies with
fluoxetine (a strong CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 inhibitor) and paroxetine (a strong CYP2D6
inhibitor). The contributions of CYP3A and CYP2C9 were confirmed with the
carbamazepine DDI study, which is a known inducer of CYP3A and CYP2C9. However, the
aficamten model with 21% contribution of CYP2D6 in its metabolism (the primary model)
resulted in overprediction of aficamten exposure in CYP2D6 PMs by 1.47-fold and
overestimation of the exposure difference between PMs and non-PMs by 1.32-fold (data not
shown). Additionally, this primary model underpredicted the interaction between aficamten
and fluconazole (Table 125). Replacing the CYP2D6 contribution with CYP2C19 (the
exploratory model) improved the prediction of aficamten exposure in CYP2D6 PMs and
interaction of aficamten with fluconazole but failed to capture the effect of paroxetine (Table
125). It is worth noting that these models’ ability to reproduce CYP2D6 polymorphism data
may not provide robust evidence for model selection, given the large prediction errors
observed in the verification of population models with CYP2D6 NM and PM phenotypes
(Figure 30). Consequently, uncertainties persist regarding the relative contributions of
CYP2D6 and CYP2C19 to aficamten metabolism.
3. In vitro studies revealed that while CYP2D6 exhibited greater catalytic activity in M1
formation (Figure 32), CYP2C19 displayed kinetic characteristics comparable to those
observed with human liver microsomes. In addition, the CYP2C19 inhibitor ticlopidine at 25
mM produced maximal inhibitory effect on M1 formation among all CYP inhibitors tested in
human liver microsomes (Figure 32). Moreover, the exploratory model, having 24%
contribution of CYP2C19 in aficamten metabolism, could reproduce the effect of fluconazole
on aficamten. Notably, fluconazole exerted markedly greater impact on aficamten
metabolism than paroxetine or fluoxetine, rendering the fluconazole DDI data more reliable
for fm verification. This is particularly relevant given that the effects of paroxetine and
fluoxetine were close to 1.25, a value that falls within the prediction error margins of PBPK
analyses. To investigate whether the underprediction of fluconazole interaction was due to an
underestimated contribution of CYP2C9, the reviewer modified the primary model by
replacing CYP2C19 clearance with CYP2C9 clearance, increasing the contribution of
CYP2C9 from approximately 56% to 67%. This adjusted model predicted a 2.56-fold
increase in aficamten AUC by fluconazole, still underestimating its actual effect. Taken
205
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
together, the prediction results using the aficamten model with increased CYP2C19
contribution warrant consideration.
4. The Applicant used the results from the primary model (Table 125) to inform the product
label. Their main argument was that the exploratory model, which did not account for
CYP2D6 metabolism, could not reproduce the paroxetine DDI results. The main assumption
of this argument is that paroxetine only inhibits CYP2D6, and the observed effect of
paroxetine on aficamten pharmacokinetics confirms the contribution of CYP2D6 in
aficamten elimination. As mentioned in Section 14.5.3.3, paroxetine is also a reversible and
time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A in vitro. The reviewer optimized the fu,mic value in the
paroxetine model so that the model could reproduce the observed interaction between
paroxetine and quinidine. To explore whether the exploratory model’s inability to predict the
paroxetine DDI with aficamten was due to an underestimation of paroxetine’s inhibitory
effects on CYP3A, the modified paroxetine model was employed to simulate the paroxetine
and aficamten DDI. The exploratory model, which has no CYP2D6 contribution, predicted
an 11% increase in aficamten AUC. The reviewer further modified the exploratory model by
assigning an fm,CYP2D6 of approximately 3% and an fm,CYP2C19 of approximately 21%. This
reviewer’s adjusted aficamten model (reviewer’s model) successfully predicted all the
changes in aficamten AUC observed in the clinical DDI studies, including paroxetine (Table
125). However, since quinidine is the only CYP3A substrate in the Certara DDI Database
that showed statistically significant interaction with paroxetine in vivo, additional simulation
was performed using the reviewer’s model and the Simcyp default paroxetine model, the one
without modification of CYP3A inhibition parameters. Paroxetine was predicted to increase
aficamten AUC by 11%, which was within 1.14-fold of the observed effect of paroxetine on
aficamten. Therefore, even without the modified inhibitory effect of paroxetine on CYP3A,
the reviewer’s aficamten model could explain all the observed DDIs with aficamten.
5. Taken together, the modeling results suggested an alternative model which assumed a higher
fm2C19 value is possible. The Applicant’s primary model (21% CYP2D6 and 3% CYP2C19)
may not fully address the clinical DDI concern especially for managing DDIs with drugs that
inhibit CYP2C19 but not CYP2D6 (e.g., fluvoxamine, fluconazole, voriconazole). For these
drugs, DDI management should be informed by the predictions using the reviewer’s model
(3% CYP2D6 and 21% CYP2C19) (Table 125). The effects of CYP2C9 or CYP2C19
phenotype on aficamten exposure were simulated and the results are shown in Table 126 and
Table 127.
6. It should be noted that the model for the CYP2C9 PM phenotype is a combination of two
distinct genotypes (*2/*3 and *3/*3), which exhibit different CYP2C9 protein metabolic
activities. The data were also weighted according to population frequencies (*2/*3: 1.6% of
total population, 84.2% of PM population; *3/*3: 0.3% of total population, 15.8% of PM
population). Consequently, the CYP2C9 PM phenotype model retains 50% of the metabolic
activity compared to the NM phenotype. Notably, the estimated difference in aficamten
exposure between CYP2C9 phenotypes may be underestimated, as the strong CYP2C9
inhibitor sulfaphenazole predicted an approximate 2-fold increase in aficamten AUC.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
Background
Aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9-mediated metabolism with contributions from
CYP3A, CYP2D6, and CYP2C19. Aficamten is primarily metabolized to two pharmacologically
inactive metabolites, CK-3834282 and CK-3834283, that circulate at approximately 56% and
103% of parent drug in plasma, respectively.
Multiple enzymes metabolizing aficamten are highly polymorphic (e.g., CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6). In the DDIs study (Study CY 601-10), only subjects identified as normal or ultrarapid
metabolizers of polymorphic enzymes were included. In addition, Study CY 6011 assessed the
effect of CYP2D6 PMs on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten (see review Section 14.2 for
additional study information and results).
The Applicant’s proposed aficamten labeling includes sections 2 DOSAGE AND
ADMINISTRATION and 7 DRUG INTERACTIONS, which describe the effect of
coadministration of inhibitors of multiple enzymatic pathways on aficamten pharmacokinetics,
efficacy, and safety, and provides recommendation for dosage adjustment. Due to lack of
availability of genetic data for CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 PMs, effect of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19
PMs in combination with inhibition of multiple enzymatic pathways, is based on the results
obtained from the clinical DDI studies and PBPK modeling. Similar increases in aficamten
exposure are expected for CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 PMs as seen with coadministration of
inhibitors of multiple enzymatic pathways that target CYP2C9 and CYP2C19. Dosage
recommendations and management of DDIs for PMs of the primary metabolizing enzyme
208
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
CYP2C9, should follow general dosage recommendation and dosage modifications for drug-drug
interactions. For additional study and PBPK modeling information refer to Section 14.2 and
Section 14.5.3.
Aficamten dosage recommendation and management of DDIs, especially in situations when
multiple metabolic pathways are inhibited, accounts for subjects who are CYP2C9 PMs with
minimal enzyme activity. No additional dosage modifications are recommended to further adjust
dosage of aficamten in subjects who are CYP2C9 PMs.
Translating CYP2C9 genotype-to-phenotype relies on categorizing alleles into functional status
based on assigned “activity value” ranging from 0 to 1 (e.g., 0 for no function, 0.5 for decreased
function, and 1.0 for normal function) which are summarized into an “activity score (AS)” for
each diplotype.

MYQORZO (aficamten)
Labeling Recommendations
Draft labeling language for 12.5 Pharmacogenomics.
12.5

Pharmacogenomics

Aficamten is extensively metabolized by multiple highly polymorphic enzymes (e.g., CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, CYP2D6) with primary metabolism mediated by CYP2C9 [see Clinical
Pharmacology (12.3)]. The impact of CYP2C9 genetic variants on the pharmacokinetics of
aficamten has not been directly evaluated, though, a similar effect on exposure is expected as
seen with strong CYP2C9 inhibitors [see Drug Interactions (7.1)].
No additional dosage modifications are required for patients who are CYP2C9 poor
metabolizers, as aficamten dosage titration and modifications account for CYP2C9
metabolizer status [see Dosage and Administration (2.2) and (2.3)].
Please refer to labeling for a final labeling recommendation.
Summary and Conclusions
Aficamten is extensively metabolized in the liver by multiple polymorphic CYP450 enzymes;
CYP2C9 (major metabolizing enzyme), CYP3A4, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. Results obtained
from the DDI studies and from the PBPK model suggest that subjects with genetically decreased
CYP enzyme activity are expected to have similar increase in exposure as those of corresponding
CYP inhibitors. Based on the available information, aficamten labeling includes
recommendations for dosage titration and dosage modification for DDIs, which accounts for
subjects who are CYP2C9 PMs.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Additional Efficacy Results in CY 6031
(SEQUOIA-HCM)
Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses for the primary endpoint ‘Change in pVO2 by CPET from baseline to Week
24’ were performed for the following subgroups:


Forest plots of results are presented in Table 131 and Table 132 below. Overall, the treatment
effect in these subgroups was consistent with that of the overall population, except in Hispanic or
Latino, and Not reported subpopulations. The largest positive treatment effect was in the
Hispanic or Latino subpopulation. Due to the very small sample size, these estimates have wide
confidence intervals and lack precision.
There were likely some random highs and random lows in sample estimates of subgroup
treatment effects due to small sample size and large variability for some subgroups. Therefore,
we also derived shrinkage estimates of subgroup treatment effects using a Bayesian hierarchical
model based on summary sample estimates. This approach leads to improved precision and
lower variability. The total variability in the sample estimates is the sum of the within subgroup
variability of the sample estimator and the across subgroups variability in underlying/true
parameter values. A shrinkage estimates of the subgroup treatment effect, which borrows
information from the other subgroups while estimating the treatment effect for a specific
subgroup, is a “weighted” average of the sample estimate and overall estimate. The weights are
based on the ratio of the between subgroup variability to the within subgroup variability. The
greater that ratio the smaller the weight on the overall estimate (the less the shrinkage). We used
the same flat prior to derive shrinkage estimates for all subgroups. The Bayesian hierarchical
model assumptions for change in pVO2 by CPET from baseline to Week 24 are:
For i =1, 2… Yi represents the observed sample estimate of the treatment effect in a subgroup
level i, assume Yi ~ N(µi, σi2) where:


σi2 are the observed variance for sample estimates



µi ~ N(µ, τ2)



µ ~ N(0, 16), τ ~ Half Normal (0,4)

We placed a prior distribution on overall mean µ ~ N(0, 16), reflecting a weakly informative
belief that the average treatment effect is centered around zero, with most of the prior mass
within ±8 units. We used a Half-Normal (0,4) prior for the standard deviation of subgroup effects
(τ). This reflects an assumption that subgroup variability is likely to be modest, based on the
observed standard errors (ranging from 0.37 to 2.1), while still allowing for larger variation if
supported by the data. Table 131 compares the conventional subgroup analysis results of the
sample estimate (in blue) and Bayesian shrinkage estimate (in red). The overall treatment effect
212
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5715605

NDA 219083
MYQORZO (aficamten)
is 1.69 (95% CI: 0.99, 2.39). Subgroup analysis using Bayesian shrinkage estimate exhibits a
narrower credible interval, and the shrinkage subgroup estimate is closer to the overall mean.

Sensitivity Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
The statistical reviewer performed sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint.
Tipping Point Analysis
For the tipping point analysis, missing pVO2 values were imputed using placebo-based multiple
imputation. Subsequently, imputed pVO2 values for subjects with missing data in the aficamten
group were systematically reduced (ranging from 90% to 26% of the imputed values) to identify
the threshold at which the treatment effect would no longer remain statistically significant. For
this analysis, imputed values in the placebo group were not shifted.
The results of this analysis (Table 133) showed that the p-value remained <0.01 through a shift
to 50% of the imputed values; and it remained <0.05 through a shift to 30% of the imputed
values. The amount by which the aficamten group shifted to lose significance seemed unlikely,
thus the results appear to be statistically robust to underlying assumptions for missing data.

MMRM Analysis
The change from baseline in pVO2 at Week 24 was analyzed using MMRM method with
stratification factors (beta-blocker use/non-use and CPET modality), visit, stratification by visit
and a numeric covariate, with 0 assigned to both treatment groups at baseline, 0 for the placebo
group at Week 24, and 1 for the aficamten group at Week 24. The results of the analysis were
consistent with the primary analysis: The treatment difference versus placebo was
1.75 mL/kg/min and nominally statistically significant (95% CI: 1.04, 2.45; p<0.0001)

17. Clinical Safety
17.1. Narratives
Narratives for Aficamten-Treated Subjects With SAE of Systolic Dysfunction or Heart
Failure
There were three SAEs of heart failure/systolic dysfunction in patients with nHCM in the OLE
Study CY 6022 at the time of the NDA submission.
(b) (6) : This subject was a 44-year-old female with a history of nHCM
Subject CY-6022and atrial fibrillation. While on a stable 20-mg dose of aficamten, they underwent an elective
pulmonary vein ablation procedure for AF at Week 28. Approximately 2 weeks later, they
presented with heart failure with LVEF of 49% (reported term: worsening heart failure and
LVEF) by site assessment and 51% by core laboratory assessment. This event was classified as a
SAE because it qualified as other medically important serious event (no hospitalization). The
aficamten dose was subsequently down-titrated to 15 mg, and their LVEF recovered to over 60%
at the next visit. While the investigator assessed the event as related to aficamten, the Applicant
attributed the cardiac failure to the recent ablation procedure, noting that heart failure is a known
complication of this intervention. The reviewer agrees that the event was confounded by the
procedure; however, the role of aficamten cannot be ruled out given that the temporal
relationship between LVEF reduction and dose up-titration (from Week 2 to Week 12) was
observed prior to the ablation procedure. It should be noted that the subject was later up-titrated
to 20 mg and continued treatment for another 40 weeks with LVEF ≥55%.

(b) (6) : This subject was a 39-year-old female with a history of nHCM
Subject CY-6022
and syncope. While on a 20-mg dose of aficamten, the subject suffered an acute thromboembolic
stroke at Week 6, followed by an SAE of persistent atrial fibrillation at Week 6 and clinical
deterioration. At Week 12, a scheduled echocardiogram revealed a significant drop in LVEF to
35% (site) and 34% (core lab), prompting an SAE report of ventricular dysfunction and the
interruption of aficamten treatment. Following a cardioversion procedure, their atrial fibrillation
resolved and their LVEF recovered to over 58%, and aficamten was restarted at a lower dose of
15 mg at Week 16. Subsequently, their clinical course was complicated by recurrent episodes of
atrial fibrillation, which caused their LVEF to fluctuate, dropping as low as 38 to 45% during
these paroxysms. The subject ultimately underwent pulmonary vein ablation around Week 53 to
manage the arrhythmia. Aficamten was interrupted at Week 60 with site LVEF of 38% with a
reported non-serious AE of ejection fraction (severe intensity). Following this interruption, the
aficamten dose was restarted and reduced to 5 mg daily from Week 62 through Week 72, during
which their LVEF remained stable in the normal range between 55% and 66%. Both the
investigator and the Applicant assessed the ventricular dysfunction as being related to the
subject's atrial fibrillation and not to the study drug.

Narratives for Aficamten-Treated Patients With Elevated Transaminases
(b) (6) : This subject was a 23-year-old White male who was on a stable
Subject CY-6031aficamten dose titrated to 10 mg by Week 8. The subject had normal liver function tests at
baseline and throughout 24 weeks of aficamten treatment. There were no AEs reported during
the treatment period. The subject experienced a transient elevation in AST (7× ULN) with mildly
elevated ALT (1.9× ULN), along with significantly elevated creatine phosphokinase (>79×
ULN) and lactate dehydrogenase (>3× ULN) during the washout period (29 days after
discontinuation of aficamten). All other liver tests were within normal range. All abnormal
parameters returned to within the normal reference range within 2 weeks without intervention.
The subject opted not to participate in the OLE Study CY 6022. Due to the absence of a temporal
relationship, the absence of increased ALP and bilirubin, and the concurrent increase of AST
with CPK and lactate dehydrogenase as well as the overall pattern of AST > ALT, this transient
increase in AST is likely of non-hepatic origin.

increase of ALT and AST (2.9× ULN and 2.7× ULN, respectively). All occurrences of mild ALT
and AST elevations returned toward baseline levels while continuing aficamten.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
The Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) conducted inspections covering the pivotal Phase 3
Trial CY 6031. Please refer to the OSI review dated July 29, 2025, for details. A summary of the
review is provided below:
OSI inspected two clinical investigator sites and the Applicant, Cytokinetics Inc. Dr. Ahmad
Masri's site (Site 1001) in Portland, Oregon, screened 29 subjects and randomized 20 subjects,
while Dr. Michael Nassif's site (Site 1009) in Kansas City, Missouri, screened 18 subjects and
randomized 12 subjects. All randomized subjects at both sites completed the study. The
inspections verified the primary efficacy endpoint data (pVO2) and found no discrepancies
between the Applicant's data listings and the sites' source documents.
The overall assessment concluded that the study appears to have been conducted adequately, and
the data generated by the clinical investigator sites and submitted by the Applicant appear
acceptable in support of the proposed indication. The Applicant's conduct and oversight of the
study were found to be adequate and compliant with Good Clinical Practice and FDA
regulations.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments


Descriptive Pregnancy Safety Study: Conduct a worldwide descriptive study that collects
prospective and retrospective data in women exposed to aficamten during pregnancy to
assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications, adverse effects on the developing fetus
and neonate, and adverse effects on the infant. Infant outcomes will be assessed through at
least the first year of life.



Lactation Study: Perform a milk-only lactation study in lactating women who have received
aficamten to measure concentrations of aficamten in breast milk using a validated assay.
Assess the effects on the breastfed infant, if available, based on study population.

Summary
In this submission the sponsor included the report of one animal carcinogenicity study in
transgenic mice. This study was intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of CK-3773274,
when administered to hemizygous transgenic rasH2 mice by oral gavage daily for 26 weeks at
appropriate dose levels.
Mouse Study:
Survival analysis:
1. The survival analyses did not show any statistically significant dose response
relationships in mortality across the vehicle control group and treated groups in either
male or female mice.
2. The survival analyses showed statistically significant increase in mortality across the
vehicle control group and positive control group in both male and female mice.
Tumor analysis:
1. For female mice, the tumor analysis did not show any tumor types with statistically
significant positive dose response relationships or statistically significant increases
in any treated group compared to the vehicle control.
2. For male mice, the trend test showed statistically significant positive dose response
relationships in the incidences of bronchioloalveolar adenoma, Lung (pvalue=0.0056<0.05) and combined tumors of bronchioloalveolar adenoma and
carcinoma (p-value=0.0212<0.05).
3. For male mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
positive control group showed statistically significant increases in the incidences of
bronchioloalveolar adenoma, lung (p-value<0.001), bronchioloalveolar carcinoma,
lung (p-value<0.001), combined tumors of bronchioloalveolar adenoma and
carcinoma, lung (p-value<0.001), hemangiosarcoma, spleen (p-value<0.001) and
combined tumors of hemangiosarcoma and hemangioma, whole body (pvalue<0.001).
4. For female mice, the pairwise comparisons between the vehicle control and the
positive control group showed statistically significant increases in the incidences of
adenoma, gland harderian (p-value=0.0052<0.05), bronchioloalveolar adenoma, lung
(p-value<0.001), bronchioloalveolar carcinoma, lung (p-value<0.001), combined
tumors of bronchioloalveolar adenoma and carcinoma, lung (p-value<0.001),

Reference ID: 5547159

NDA219083

Page 5 of 22

hemangioma, spleen (p-value=0.0498<0.05), hemangiosarcoma, spleen (pvalue<0.001) and combined tumors of hemangiosarcoma and hemangioma, whole
body (p-value<0.001).

Page 6 of 22

1. Background
In this submission the sponsor included the report of one animal carcinogenicity study in
transgenic mice. This study was intended to assess the carcinogenic potential of CK-3773274,
when administered by oral gavage daily for 26 weeks at appropriate dose levels.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as the dose increases.

2. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in males and one in females. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one
positive control group. Two hundred and fifty hemizygous transgenic rasH2 mice of each sex
were randomly assigned to the treated groups vehicles and positive control groups in equal
size of 25 mice per group. The mice in the vehicle control groups received 10% (w/w)
Polysorbate 80 in Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI) Water. The mice in the water control
group received RODI Water. The positive control mice were dosed by intraperitoneal
injections with Urethane in 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection, USP, once on Days 1, 3, and
5. The study was designed to continue for up to 26 weeks for both sexes, however in
accordance with study termination criteria, all surviving mice were sacrificed during Week
27. The oveall study design and dose levels are shown in Table 1.
